Case 19-M-0133 - In the Matter of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation’s Notice of Pension 
Settlement Loss and Request for Waiver of 60-Day Filing Requirement. 
 
 
STAFF STATEMENT IN SUPPORT OF THE JOINT PROPOSAL 
I. 
INTRODUCTION 
 
 
On September 27, 2021, Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
(Niagara Mohawk or the Company), Staff of the New York State Department of Public Service 
(Staff), and Multiple Intervenors (MI), the New York Power Authority (NYPA), Direct Energy 
Services LLC, and Marathon Power LLC (collectively the Signatory Parties) submitted a Joint 
Proposal recommending a comprehensive resolution of all issues raised in the above-captioned 
proceedings.  By this Statement in Support of the Joint Proposal (Initial Statement), Staff 
recommends that the Commission adopt the provisions of the Joint Proposal and establish three-
year electric and gas rate plans for Niagara Mohawk as of July 1, 2021. 
II. 
PROCEDURAL HISTORY 
 
 
On July 31, 2020, the Company filed tariff leaves, direct testimony, and exhibits 
in support of their request to increase electric and gas delivery rates for the rate year ending 
June 30, 2022, referred to in the context of the proposed three-year rate plans as “RY1.” 
 
 
On September 1, 2020, Administrative Law Judges (ALJs) James A. Costello and 
Ashley Moreno presided over a procedural conference and technical conference in these 
proceedings.  Thereafter, the ALJs issued a ruling providing a procedural schedule for these 


--- Page 9 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
2 
cases.1  The ruling provided that: the Company would file corrections and updates on 
October 14, 2020 and discovery-related exhibits on October 21, 2020; Staff and intervenors 
would file testimony and exhibits on November 25, 2020 and discovery-related exhibits on 
December 2, 2020; all parties would file rebuttal testimony and exhibits on December 16, 2020 
and discovery-related exhibits on December 23, 2020; and the evidentiary hearing would 
commence on January 6, 2021. 
 
 
On October 14, 2020, the Company filed its Corrections and Updates (C&U) 
testimony and exhibits that resulted in an increase of $2.913 million in its electric revenue 
requirement request and a decrease of $4.684 in its gas revenue requirement request.  Niagara 
Mohawk stated that these changes were the results of updates to revenue forecasts, capital 
investment plans and operations and maintenance (O&M) expenses. 
 
 
On November 25, 2020, Staff filed direct testimony and exhibits and proposed a 
RY1 electric delivery revenue increase of $30.5 million (a decrease of $133.8 million from the 
Company’s position), and a RY1 gas delivery revenue decrease of $7.0 million (a decrease of 
$44.1 million from the Company’s position).  On or around November 25, 2020, the following 
intervenors also filed direct testimony and exhibits: NYPA; MI; the Natural Resources Defense 
Council (NRDC) and the Sierra Club; Citizen Action of New York, Inc. (Citizen Action); 
Department of State, Division of Consumer Protection, Utility Intervention Unit (UIU); Public 
Utility Law Project of New York, Inc. (PULP); Stop NY Fracked Gas Pipeline (Stop); the 
Environmental Defense Fund (EDF); Robert Wyman; the Alliance for a Green Economy 
(AGREE) and the Pace Energy and Climate Center (Pace).  Exhibits containing responses to 
discovery requests were filed on their respective dates.  Niagara Mohawk, Staff, the Coalition for 
Renewable Natural Gas, NRDC and Sierra Club, Citizen Action, MI, and UIU filed rebuttal 
testimony on December 16, 2020. 
 
 
On December 9, 2020, Niagara Mohawk filed a Notice of Impending Settlement 
Negotiations, stating that settlement discussions would begin on December 22, 2020.  The 
Company then filed a Revised Notice of Impending Settlement Negotiations, postponing the date 
for the initial settlement discussion meeting to January 5, 2021.  On December 22, 2020, Niagara 
Mohawk filed a request to extend the rate suspension period to July 31, 2021 provided that a

disadvantaged, which was subsequently approved by the Commission.2  On the same date, the 
ALJs issued a ruling postponing the hearing until a future date.  The first settlement session was 
held on January 5, 2021, with subsequent sessions held on various dates with notice provided to 
all parties to the proceedings. 
 
 
On February 22, 2021, Niagara Mohawk filed a consent to further extend the 
maximum suspension period through October 1, 2021, subject to a make whole provision.  On 
March 15, 2021, Niagara Mohawk filed a third Notice of Impending Settlement Negotiations to 
include a Niagara Mohawk petition requesting modification to its incremental residential 
methane detection program.3  On May 5, 2021, Niagara Mohawk submitted a letter requesting an 
extension of the suspension period, with a make whole provision, through November 30, 2021.  
On June 22, 2021, the Commission approved the extension of the maximum suspension period 
through November 30, 2021 and granted the Company’s request for a make whole provision as if 
the ultimately approved rates went into effect on July 1, 2021.4 
 
 
On July 9, 2021, Niagara Mohawk filed a fourth Notice of Impending Settlement 
Negotiations to include a Niagara Mohawk petition requesting authority to defer an actuarial 
experience pension settlement loss for the fiscal year ending March 31, 2019.5  On July 21, 2021, 
Niagara Mohawk submitted a further consent to extend the maximum suspension period through 
December 31, 2021, subject to a make whole provision.  On August 13, 2021, the Company 
submitted a further consent to an additional extension of the suspension period, with a make 
whole provision, through February 28, 2022. 
III. 
OVERVIEW OF THE JOINT PROPOSAL 
 
 
As discussed in greater detail below, the Joint Proposal contains a number of 
provisions designed to provide for just and reasonable rates, particularly in light of the impacts

the COVID-19 pandemic has had on ratepayers; maintain and improve Niagara Mohawk’s 
ability to provide safe and adequate service; and to the extent feasible and appropriate, provide a 
resolution to other issues raised by intervenors.  Section I of the Joint Proposal provides the 
procedural background for the proceedings addressed in the Joint Proposal.  Section II provides 
the overall framework for the Joint Proposal.  Section III provides definitions, including for the 
rate years: July 1, 2021 through June 30, 2022 (RY1); July 1, 2022 through June 30, 2023 
(RY2); and July 1, 2023 through June 30, 2024 (RY3) (collectively, Rate Years).  Section III 
also defines “Stayout Period” as the period beginning July 1, 2024 and ending on the earlier of 
the effective date of revised base rates for Niagara Mohawk or March 31, 2025.  Sections IV 
presents the rate plans for Niagara Mohawk’s electric and gas businesses. 
 
 
Turning to the provisions of Section IV, Subsection 1 sets forth the effective date 
(July 1, 2021), and term (July 1, 2021 through June 30, 2024) of the rate plans.  Subsection 2 
provides the electric and gas revenue requirements, the return on equity (ROE), the treatment of 
excess deferred taxes, the use of credits owed to customers, and the make whole provision.  
Subsection 3 concerns the electric revenue forecast, revenue allocation and rate design, and the 
earnings adjustment mechanism surcharge.  This subsection also details various tariff revisions 
required for electric service.  Subsection 4 addresses the gas revenue forecast, revenue allocation 
and rate design, the gas safety and reliability surcharge, required tariff changes, and lost and 
unaccounted for gas. 
 
 
Subsection 5 addresses the disposition of excess earnings, calculation of the 
Company’s actual ROE after each rate year, and the details of the earnings sharing mechanism.  
Subsection 6 details the Company’s electric capital investments and infrastructure programs, 
including the Gilmantown energy storage project.  Subsection 7 addresses gas capital investment 
and infrastructure, including withdrawal of the application for a certificate to construct and 
operate the Albany Loop Project, service lines, meter related issues, first responder training, 
leakage inspections, and safety programs. 
 
 
Subsection 8 addresses advanced metering infrastructure (AMI) issues, including 
installation and opt-out procedures.  Subsection 9 concerns programs related to electric vehicles.  
Subsection 10 addresses information technology issues, including capital budgets, specific 
programs, and reporting requirements.  Subsection 11 addresses streetlighting issues such as 
pricing, service charges, sales, maintenance, attachments to utility poles, and tariff changes.  


--- Page 12 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
5 
Subsection 12 discusses new and existing reconciliations, deferrals, and true-ups for Niagara 
Mohawk, including pensions and OPEBs, energy affordability, economic development. site 
investigation and remediation, property tax expenses, revenue adjustments, major and minor 
storm expenses, and various fees. 
 
 
Subsection 13 addresses the Company’s service quality assurance program, 
including COVID-19’s impacts on the program’s customer service metrics, uncollectible 
expense, and elimination of the gas cost estimating metric.  Subsection 14 concerns gas safety 
metrics, leak prone pipe (LPP) removal, the related incentive program and reporting 
requirements, leak management, damage prevention, and emergency response.  Subsection 15 
addresses customer programs including energy affordability, collections and protections, 
outreach and education, submetering, arrears resolution, and language access.  Subsection 16 
addresses earnings adjustment mechanisms.  Subsection 17 concerns non-pipe alternatives, 
energy efficiency and related matters.  Specifically, the subsection addresses gas demand 
response, locally produced methane, a Multi-use Hydrogen Production and Utilization Facility in 
Ithaca, economic development programs, and energy efficiency programs.  Subsection 18 
addresses efforts to reduce gas usage, and non-gas heating options.  Finally, Subsections 19 
through 21 address administrative issues including post-plan treatment of some elements, 
corporate structure and affiliate rules, and standard provisions common to all joint proposals. 
IV. 
THE JOINT PROPOSAL IS IN THE PUBLIC INTEREST 
1. 
Benefits of the Joint Proposal 
 
 
The Joint Proposal provides various benefits to ratepayers.  First, the plan 
provides for rate stability over its duration, with total revenue percent increases, after the use of 
credits, of less than two percent for both electric and gas service for each of the Rate Years.  
Further, the RY1 proposed increases of $49.4 million and $12.5 million for electric and gas, 
respectively are significantly lower than those requested by the Company in its testimony.  The 
overall revenue requirement increases reflect a reasonable compromise between the Company’s, 
Staff’s, and other parties’ litigation positions.  Additionally, the impact of the revenue 
requirement increases on customers has been mitigated through the use of existing regulatory 
liabilities to offset revenue increases.  The measured, multi-year rates set forth in the Joint 
Proposal would not be achievable in a litigated outcome. 


--- Page 13 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
6 
 
 
Additionally, the Joint Proposal provides multiple non-monetary benefits while 
advancing important Commission and State priorities.  First, it continues to ensure that the 
Company is capable of providing safe and adequate service by funding necessary safety 
programs related to vegetation management6 and storm response7 for electric service, and LPP 
removal,8 methane detection programs,9 and meter relocations10 for gas service.  The Joint 
Proposal continues the Commission-approved transition to AMI that will greatly enhance 
ratepayers’ ability to monitor and control their energy consumption on a real time basis, which 
will enhance energy efficiency efforts across the Company’s service territory. 
 
 
The Joint Proposal refines protocols for municipalities that seek to purchase 
streetlighting facilities to switch to light emitting diode (LED) fixtures and for parties seeking to 
install chargers for electric vehicles.  These two provisions will contribute to the State’s 
environmental and electrification goals, as will the Joint Proposal’s sections clarifying the 
treatment of non-wire alternatives11 in the Company’s capital planning. 
 
 
Regarding gas service, the Joint Proposal furthers the State’s environmental goals 
by requiring the consideration of non-pipe alternatives to ameliorate the need for LPP 
replacements,12 requiring the Company to present information on clean energy heating 
alternatives to potential gas customers,13 ending the marketing of gas services,14 and enhancing 
energy efficiency programs.15 
 
 
Finally, the Joint Proposal provides consumer protections by expanding the 
enrollment and benefits of its energy affordability program,16 and enhancing extreme weather 
protections.17

The many of the features cited above, and others detailed below, would not have 
been achievable through litigation.  The Joint Proposal is an over-all benefit to ratepayers and its 
terms should be adopted by the Commission. 
2. 
Standard of Review 
 
 
The Commission’s Settlement Guidelines state that all decisions, including those 
to adopt the terms of settlement agreements (joint proposals) must be just and reasonable and in 
the public interest.18  In addition to compliance with proper procedures, determining whether the 
terms of a joint proposal are in the public interest involves substantive consideration of the 
following: 
1. consistency with the law and regulatory economic, social and environmental 
State and Commission policies; 
2. whether the terms of the joint proposal compare favorably with the likely result 
of a fully litigated case and produce a result within the range of reasonable 
outcomes; 
3. whether the joint proposal fairly balances the interests of ratepayers, investors 
and the long-term soundness of the utility; and 
4. whether the joint proposal provides a rational basis for the Commission’s 
decision.19 
3. 
The Joint Proposal Comports with the Commission’s Settlement Guidelines 
 
 
The Joint Proposal entered into in these cases resolves all outstanding issues 
presented in testimony and settlement negotiations.  In doing so, it fully comports with the 
Commission’s Settlement Guidelines.  The fact that intervening parties representing large 
commercial customers, NYPA, an affected labor union, and energy service companies (ESCOs) 
are signatories to the Joint Proposal shows that the document balances the parties’ diverse 
interests while ensuring Niagara Mohawk’s continued delivery of safe, adequate, and reliable 
electric and gas service at just and reasonable rates.  In addition to the parties that signed the 
Joint Proposal, Staff notes that a number of parties have stated that they will not oppose the Joint 
Proposal.

3.1 
The Joint Proposal is Consistent with State Policies 
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends outcomes that are consistent with the State’s and 
Commission’s policies.  The recommended outcomes ensure that Niagara Mohawk will continue 
to provide safe and adequate service at just and reasonable rates.  Specifically, the terms of the 
Joint Proposal will continue Niagara Mohawk’s efforts to retire LPP in its service territory, 
enhancing the safety of the communities served by the Company.  Additionally, the rate mitigation 
recommended in the Joint Proposal helps to shield customers from significant rate increases during 
RY1, RY2, and RY3, while the economy in the Company’s service territory continues to recover 
from the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic.  Regarding environmental matters, the Joint Proposal 
is consistent with the Commission’s energy efficiency policies as set forth in its order regarding New 
Efficiency: New York (NE:NY),20 and also with the Climate Leadership and Community Protection 
Act (CLCPA). 
3.2 
The Joint Proposal is Consistent with the CLCPA 
 
 
The CLCPA enacted Article 75 of the New York Environmental Conservation 
Law (ECL).  ECL §75-0107 sets statewide greenhouse gas emissions limits of 60 percent of 
1990 emissions levels by 2030 and 15 percent of 1990 emissions levels by 2050.  Further, that 
provision requires the New York State Department of Environmental Conservation (DEC) to 
promulgate rules and regulations setting the greenhouse gas emissions limits in terms of carbon 
dioxide equivalents.  Additionally, the CLCPA, provides that state agencies must “consider 
whether [their] decisions are inconsistent with or will interfere with the attainment of the 
statewide greenhouse gas emissions limits established in [ECL §75-0107].”  Where a decision is 
found to be inconsistent with the greenhouse gas emissions limits, an agency must “provide a 
detailed statement of justification as to why such limits/criteria may not be met, and identify 
alternatives or greenhouse gas mitigation measures to be required where such project is 
located.”21 
 
 
Complete CLCPA regulations have not yet been promulgated.  At present, the 
DEC has promulgated 6 NYCRR Part 496, which sets forth the top-level statewide greenhouse 
gas emissions limits for 2030 and 2050 in terms of tons of carbon dioxide equivalents.  Details of 
 
20  Case 18-M-0084, New Efficiency: New York, Order Authorizing Utility Energy Efficiency and 
Building Electrification Portfolios Through 2025 (issued January 16, 2020) (NE:NY Order). 
21 CLCPA §7. 


--- Page 16 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
9 
implementing the CLCPA are presently being discussed before the Climate Action Council, 
which is expected to issue a draft scoping plan by the end of 2021 “outlining the 
recommendations for attaining the statewide greenhouse gas emissions limits in accordance with 
the schedule established in section 75–0107 of this article, and for the reduction of emissions 
beyond eighty-five percent, net zero emissions in all sectors of the economy….”22  That draft 
scoping plan will be subject to public comment and the Climate Action Council will then issue a 
final scoping plan by the end of 2022.23 
 
 
In this context of early implementation of the CLCPA, the Commission has ruled 
on the applicability of CLCPA to rate proceedings.  First, in Corning Natural Gas Corporation’s 
(Corning) recent rate proceeding,24 Corning proposal to accelerate deprecation of its gas 
distribution infrastructure so that the company received the full return of its investment by 2050, 
reasoning that the CLCPA will render these assets valueless by 2050.  The Commission rejected 
this proposal, determining that it was impossible to know in 2020 what Corning’s business will 
look like in 2050.  Further, the Commission found that there is presently great uncertainty as to 
how the CLPCA goals will be met and the impact that may have on the value of Corning’s gas 
distribution system.  The Commission argued that the climate goals could be achieved without 
affecting gas distribution or that the infrastructure could be repurposed and retain all or a portion 
of its value.  Given these possibilities, the Commission found Corning’s proposal premature. 
 
 
Second, regarding gas rates for Niagara Mohawk’s downstate affiliates, The 
Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (KEDNY) and KeySpan Gas East Corp. 
d/b/a National Grid (KEDLI),25 the Commission considered arguments from third parties that 
projected increases in gas sales and spending on gas infrastructure were contrary to the CLCPA’s 
requirements.  The Commission found that §§7(2) and (3) applied to rate proceedings, which are 
“other administrative approvals” under the statute.  Concerning the substance of the joint 
proposal in those cases, the Commission determined that the requirements of the CLCPA do not 
exist in a vacuum and must be interpreted consistently with the Commission’s statutory duty to

ensure safe and adequate service.26  In this context, the Commission found the joint proposal to 
be consistent with the CLCPA even though some proposed capital projects would meet increased 
peak customer demand for gas because these projects were needed to ensure continued safe, 
reliable, and adequate service to ratepayers. 
 
 
Under this framework, the Joint Proposal in these cases clearly meets the 
requirements of the CLCPA.  While as an electric and gas utility the Company must by law 
provide service to all who request it, the Joint Proposal commits Niagara Mohawk to reducing 
the environmental impact of the utility service it provides to customers.  As detailed further 
below, the Company will pursue policies designed to reduce expansion of gas heating while 
ensuring the safety and reliability of the existing gas distribution infrastructure.  Regarding 
electric service, Niagara Mohawk will work to expand electrification and energy efficiency.  
Taken as a whole, the Joint Proposal will contribute to the goals of the CLCPA while satisfying 
the Company’s obligations under the PSL to provide safe and adequate service to its ratepayers. 
 
 
Regarding gas sales, the Joint Proposal forecasts increases in non-generator gas 
sales in RY1, RY2, and RY3.  These forecasts are not goals or targets in the sense of a desired 
outcome, but rather a best estimate of customer usage that can be expected based on the available 
data and economic variables.  In recognition of the need to decrease natural gas consumption to 
meet the CLCPA’s emissions targets, particularly by 2050 the Joint Proposal commits Niagara 
Mohawk to work to reduce total gas sales from forecasted levels by one percent in RY2 and 
RY3, effectively flattening non-generation gas sales at the RY1 level.27  Staff recognizes this is a 
first step, but an important one, to changing the trajectory of gas usage in Niagara Mohawk’s 
service territory.  Niagara Mohawk will endeavor to achieve these goals through various means, 
including: energy efficiency and demand response programs, the use of non-gas non-pipe 
alternatives for some LPP replacements and customer connections, the promotion of geothermal 
and electric heating options to new customers,28 and a $1 million shareholder-funded gas-to-heat 
pump conversion program.29  In addition to these efforts, Niagara Mohawk will eliminate all 
promotional and marketing programs related to the expansion of gas service and its future energy

28 Id.

efficiency marketing will encourage electrification and not mention any environmental benefits 
of natural gas.30  To this end, the Company will develop educational material informing 
customers of alternatives to gas heating, such as heat pumps, and highlight existing incentives for 
conversion while providing information on entities performing installations in its service 
territory and link to the New York State Energy Research and Development Authority’s 
(NYSERDA) heat pump website.31 
 
 
The Company’s LPP program will also contribute to the CLCPA’s goals, most 
directly by reducing the amount of gas escaping from the Company’s infrastructure, but also 
through the Joint Proposal’s requirement to consider non-pipe alternatives (NPA) when replacing 
LPP.  Specifically, Niagara Mohawk will identify sections of LPP that can be eliminated through 
the use of “shared geothermal loops or individual ground- or air-source heat pumps” for affected 
customers.32  The Company will also commit to addressing five LPP segments through removal 
of the infrastructure, where cost-effective. 
 
 
Other provisions in the Joint Proposal also work to reduce reliance on gas heat.  
For new customer connections serving five or more ratepayers and involving a main extension 
greater than 500 feet, Niagara Mohawk will perform an analysis to see if the ratepayers could be 
efficiently served by non-gas heat and present the options to the prospective customers, stressing 
the environmental benefits of the alternatives.33 
 
 
Additionally, the Joint Proposal addresses Niagara Mohawk’s currently pending 
Article VII application for the Pipeline E37 Reliability and Resiliency Project, also known as the 
Albany Loop Project, which would increase gas availability in the Capital Region.34  As part of 
the Joint Proposal, Niagara Mohawk has agreed to withdraw the application, and not reapply for 
the duration of the proposed rate plan.35

Finally, to ensure the various environmental efforts are effective, the Joint 
Proposal requires the Company to provide significant reports regarding its gas system, including: 
(1) semi-annual filings reporting on monthly billed gas use by residential, commercial, and 
industrial sector, customer counts and monthly net changes, and the results of the Company’s 
initiatives to influence billed gas usage;36 (2) a report, to be filed before the end of RY2, 
assessing the energy efficiency and other non-infrastructure programs necessary to achieve 
climate appropriate reductions in billed gas usage in future years;37 (3) semi-annual reports 
regarding NPA projects to remove LPP rather than replace it;38 and (4) in its next rate filing, a 
calculation of its gas system’s 1990 GHG emissions levels, GHG emissions levels at the time of 
its filing, and an assessment of the impact of the rate filing on GHG emissions, by program and 
investment.39 
 
 
Regarding the electric business, the Joint Proposal will establish a surcharge to 
recover costs related to the Company’s Electric Vehicle Program, which will facilitate the build 
out of infrastructure needed to expand the use of electric vehicles.  The Joint Proposal also 
continues the Company’s street-light program initiatives, which encourage the conversion to 
energy efficient LED technology by both Niagara Mohawk and municipalities that choose to 
own their own streetlighting facilities.40 Additionally, the Joint Proposal allows for the automatic 
substitution of failed luminaries with LED replacements.41 
 
 
Across both the electric and gas businesses, the Joint Proposal provides cost 
recovery to enable the implementation of NE:NY energy efficiency programs.  Further, the it 
provides flexibility for Niagara Mohawk to meet any modified energy efficiency targets that may 
be established when the Commission completes its NE:NY midpoint review.42 
 
 
While the Joint Proposal is intended to establish rates for electric and gas service 
for a utility required to provide those services to all applicants, the proposed provisions will 
result in the more efficient use of existing electric and gas infrastructure while endeavoring to

requirement to provide safe and adequate service at just and reasonable rates. 
3.3 
The Joint Proposal Compares Favorably to the Litigation Positions of the 
Active Parties to These Proceedings 
 
 
Comparing the litigation positions of the Active Parties to the terms of the Joint 
Proposal supports the conclusion that the Joint Proposal produces a result within the range that 
could be expected in litigation.43  Indeed, as explained above, the total percent revenue increases 
are below two percent for both the electric and gas business for each of the Rate Years of the rate 
plan.  In addition, the Joint Proposal’s proposed revenue requirement increases for RY1 of $49.4 
million and $12.5 million for electric and gas, respectively, are significantly lower than the 
$108.9 million and $37.6 million requested by the Company.44  Further, the Joint Proposal 
contains various provisions that place a strong emphasis on the Company managing its costs, and 
provides incentives to that end (e.g., net-plant reconciliation, the gas safety performance metrics, 
and customer service performance mechanism).  At the same time, Niagara Mohawk will receive 
sufficient additional revenues enabling it to implement new programs and maintain its electric 
and gas systems to ensure the continued provision of safe, adequate, and reliable service.  
Moreover, the Joint Proposal continues reconciliation mechanisms and reporting requirements 
characteristic of Commission-adopted rate plans.   
The Joint Proposal’s recommended allowed ROE of 9.0 percent is a fair 
compromise between the Company’s and Staff’s filed positions of 9.5 percent and 8.7 percent, 
respectively.  Additionally, the 9.0 percent ROE is comparable to, though slightly higher than, 
the ROE allowed for other major utilities operating under recent Commission-approved multi-
year rate plans.45  Moreover, the 9.0 percent ROE for a multi-year rate plan compares favorably 
to the 8.8 percent ROE for a single rate year adopted by the Commission in May 2021 for

Mohawk were to over-earn.  
3.4 
The Joint Proposal Appropriately Balances the Interests of Stakeholders 
 
 
This proceeding has seen the participation of parties representing multiple 
interests: large industrial customers, ESCOs, consumer advocates, environmental advocates, 
geothermal heating providers, and municipal entities.  Fourteen parties in addition to the 
Company and Staff submitted testimony.  Many parties actively participated in settlement 
negotiations.  As a result of this participation, the Joint Proposal accommodates the interests of 
the various parties. 
 
 
This balancing can be seen in the low annual rate increases for both gas and 
electric service, which will avoid significant rate increases for ratepayers, especially those on a 
fixed income or still feeling the economic effects of the COVID-19 pandemic, while still 
ensuring that the Company will have sufficient funds to provide safe and adequate service. 
 
 
The treatment of gas service and infrastructure also shows the efforts taken to 
balance parties’ interests in the Joint Proposal.  While Niagara Mohawk will continue the work 
of enhancing safety and reducing gas loss through the replacement of LPP, it will also explore 
NPAs to replacement.  For example, it could convince customers served by a segment of LPP to 
convert to electric heating and appliances, enabling the Company to abandon that segment of 
LPP rather than replace it.  Also, as an accommodation to the environmental advocates, the 
Company will discontinue its gas marketing to prospective customers48 and providing 
information on no-fossil fuel heating alternatives.49 
 
 
The Joint Proposal also establishes a surcharge for capital projects related to 
electric vehicle charging infrastructure, which will enable Niagara Mohawk to advance the 
installation of such infrastructure, helping the State to reach its environmental goals in the

coming years.  Also, the Joint Proposal refines the process by which municipalities can purchase 
street lighting infrastructure in order to convert to efficient LED fixtures, which addresses the 
interests of the municipalities and advances environmental goals. 
3.5 
The Commission has a Rational Basis on which to Adopt the Terms of the 
Joint Proposal 
 
 
As discussed above, the parties to these proceedings have had the opportunity to 
file multiple rounds of testimony and exhibits.  Fourteen parties have done so.  This pre-filed 
testimony thoroughly identifies the basis for parties’ litigation positions in these cases.  
Accordingly, these cases present the Commission with a practically complete litigation record. 
The Joint Proposal provides for the resolution of all of the issues raised in these cases in a 
reasonable manner, reflecting parties’ positions in litigation and compromises between those 
positions.  All parties to these cases had the opportunity to participate in the settlement 
negotiations and many parties actively participated in the settlement negotiations process.  
Moreover, the parties have the opportunity to explain their positions with regard to the Joint 
Proposal in the Initial and Reply Statements.  Accordingly, there is an ample record with 
sufficient support for the Commission to have a rational basis on which to adopt the terms of the 
Joint Proposal. 
 
 
In sum, the Joint Proposal should be adopted because it satisfies the criteria the 
Commission has established, pursuant to the PSL, for judging the reasonableness of proposed 
settlements, namely that they set forth terms that enable the continued provision of safe and 
adequate service at just and reasonable rates.  Furthermore, the Joint Proposal fairly balances 
parties’ interests on the issues presented in these cases and produces results that may not have 
been attainable except through a joint proposal. 
 
 
Additionally, as part of the Joint Proposal, the Company has agreed to submit 
various reports and convene meetings with Staff and other interested parties on a variety of 
issues, including CLCPA compliance, and progress toward limiting gas usage.  These reports and 
meetings will enable parties to engage with the Company during the term of the proposed rate 
plan and in advance of its next base rate filing.  The Signatory Parties recognize the importance 
of an open exchange of ideas and information with respect to these topics.  These collaborative 
processes ensure that the Company, Staff, and other interested parties will have the opportunity 
to work together to develop mutually beneficial projects and ideas. 


--- Page 23 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
16 
 
 
The Joint Proposal is a detailed proposal to the Commission submitted by the 
Signatory Parties as to the costs and revenues underlying the proposed base rates and 
mechanisms provided for in the Joint Proposal.  These costs and revenues, along with the other 
terms of the Joint Proposal, provide a sound, equitable, and rational evidentiary basis on which to 
determine that the provisions of the Joint Proposal are reasonable and, therefore, should be 
adopted. 
 
 
When considering whether the Joint Proposal is in the public interest, the 
document should be considered as a whole, with each individual provision providing support and 
balance to the others.  Staff is aware that the Commission may accept, reject, or modify, in whole 
or in part, any recommendation or term of the Joint Proposal; however, it is Staff’s belief that the 
Joint Proposal fairly resolves the ratemaking and policy initiatives of the Commission, thereby 
providing improved service at an equitable and well-reasoned cost.  The Joint Proposal meets the 
public interest standard and, thus, should be adopted. 
 
 
The record is more than adequate to support the terms of the Joint Proposal, 
which are consistent with both law and policy, have a rational basis, balance the interests of 
customers and the Company, and compare favorably with the outcome of litigation.  For these 
reasons, the Joint Proposal should be adopted. 
V. 
ELEMENTS OF THE JOINT PROPOSAL50 
1. 
Effective Date and Term 
 
 
As explained above, the Joint Proposal recommends three-year rate plans for 
Niagara Mohawk’s electric and gas businesses.  The effective date, i.e., the beginning of RY1, is 
July 1, 2021.  However, the Signatory Parties recognize that the beginning of RY1 has now 
passed.  Accordingly, due to the extended period of negotiations, Niagara Mohawk will recover 
the revenue it would have collected since July 1, 2021 and the actual date of implementation of 
new rates through its various reconciliation mechanisms.  The rates set forth in the Joint Proposal 
presume that the implementation of new rates will occur on January 1, 2022.  Should that not 
 
50 To facilitate the reader’s comparison of the actual provisions of the Joint Proposal with the

headings in Section IV of the Joint Proposal.

occur, additional measures would need to be taken to effectuate the make whole provision 
through the actual date new rates go into operation. 
2. 
Electric and Gas Revenue Requirements 
 
 
Staff, in its review of the Company’s filings and responses to discovery requests, 
made every effort to ensure that the electric and gas rate revenue requirements were no higher 
than necessary to enable the utility to recover its costs as well as the opportunity to earn a fair 
return on its investment.  The Joint Proposal limits the total revenue increases to 1.4 percent for 
electric and 1.8 percent for gas in RY1 and 1.9 percent increases in RY2 and RY3 for both the 
electric and gas businesses.  To limit the impact of the Company’s rate requests on customers, 
and in recognition of the financial impacts on customers associated with the COVID-19 
pandemic, the Joint Proposal includes specific adjustments to the revenue requirement increases 
the Company requested in litigation.  Some of these adjustments include but are not limited to: 
(1) limiting management wage increases; (2) reducing capital expenditures (CapEx); (3) 
reducing the number of incremental full time equivalent employees (FTEs); (4) reducing O&M 
expense associated with new programs; (5) deferring and delaying new or enhanced programs; 
(6) reducing information technology expenditures; and (7) using existing deferred regulatory 
liabilities to further reduce the impact of the resulting revenue requirement increases on 
customers.  The revenue requirement increases result in an appropriate amount of funds to allow 
the Company to continue providing safe and adequate service to customers, and at the same time, 
make progress towards various policy goals and objectives.  Further, the revenue requirement 
increases are proposed in tandem with a several reconciliation or true-up mechanisms designed 
to minimize the risk of the Company over/under spending on any expense items, as well as 
various performance metrics to ensure that the Company provides both safe and quality service 
to its customers.  The tables below illustrate the RY1 – RY3 revenue requirement increases for 
electric and gas, adjusted for rate compression, assuming an effective date of January 1, 2022, as 
well as the offsetting use of net deferred credits:

in the state income tax rate from 6.5 percent to 7.25 percent, which increases the revenue 
requirement by $3.0 million.  Appendix A to this Statement identifies all the corrections and 
updates Staff would have made to its pre-filed position if this rate proceeding was fully litigated.  
Staff’s corrected and updated position would result in a revenue requirement decrease of $5.6 
million. 
 
 
The difference between Staff’s revenue requirement decrease of $5.6 million, and 
the Joint Proposal’s recommended increase of $49.4 million is approximately $54.9 million.  
This $54.9 million difference primarily results from adjustments to the following areas: net 
margin, an increase of $0.6 million, which results in a decrease to the revenue requirement; 
O&M expenses, an increase of $29.5 million; depreciation expense, an increase of $9.9 million; 
ROE, an increase from 8.7 percent to 9.0 percent, or $12.7 million; and rate base, an increase of 
$1.2 million. 
 
 
The change in net margin from Staff’s litigated position to the Joint Proposal is 
primarily the result of refinement in the priceout from Staff’s direct testimony.  The Joint 
Proposal includes a more granular allocation of Staff’s sales forecast.  The differences regarding 
O&M expenses are related to four main areas: minor storms, $13.3 million; labor expense, $5.3 
million; other initiatives, $3.9 million; and transportation, $1.7 million.  The main difference 
regarding depreciation expense is due to the inclusion of an amortization for Automatic Meter 
Reading (AMR) devices.

RY2 and $109.8 million in RY3.  As shown in Appendix 1, Schedule 1 of the Joint Proposal, the 
incremental revenue requirements for both RY2 and RY3 reflect changes in net margin, 
increases in various expenses associated with inflation or with specific forecasts, increases in 
depreciation expense, increases in property tax expense, and increases in rate base for net utility 
plant resulting from forecasted capital spending.

In pre-filed testimony, the Company requested a revenue requirement increase of 
$37.6 million for RY1.54  As set forth in testimony and exhibits, Staff recommended a revenue 
requirement decrease of $7.0 million for RY1.55  Staff premised its recommendation on an 8.7 
percent ROE and a 7.40 percent overall pre-tax rate of return.56  Subsequent to Staff filing its 
testimony, several corrections and updates have been identified, which include: a correction to 
net margin, which decreases the revenue requirement by $0.7 million; several corrections to 
O&M expenses, which increase the revenue requirement by $1.3 million; an update to property 
taxes, which increases the revenue requirement by $1.3 million; and an update in the state 
income tax rate from 6.5 percent to 7.25 percent, which increases the revenue requirement by 
$0.7 million.  Appendix B to this Statement identifies all the corrections and updates Staff would 
have made to its pre-filed position if this rate proceeding was fully litigated.  Staff’s corrected 
and updated position would result in a revenue requirement decrease of $4.5 million. 
 
 
The difference between Staff’s revenue requirement decrease of $4.5 million and 
the Joint Proposal’s recommended increase of $12.5 million is approximately $17 million.  This 
$17 million difference primarily results from adjustments to the following areas: O&M expenses, 
an increase of $4.9 million; amortization of regulatory deferrals, a decrease of $3.1 million; 
depreciation expense, an increase of $11 million; ROE, an increase from 8.7 percent to 9.0 
percent, or $3.1 million; and rate base, an increase of $0.4 million. 
 
 
The differences regarding O&M expenses are related to four main areas: other 
initiatives, $1.7 million; labor expense, $1.2 million; contractors, $0.8 million; and 
transportation, $0.4 million.  The difference regarding amortization of regulatory deferrals is due 
to the acceleration of the LPP amortization.  The main difference regarding depreciation expense 
is related to the annual amortization of $2 million of costs associated with AMR-associated 
electronic remote transmitters and the annual amortization of $8.3 million of costs associated 
with LPP.

(c) 
Cost of Capital 
 
 
In Niagara Mohawk’s pre-filed testimony, it requested an overall after-tax cost of 
capital of 6.32 percent for RY1, which was predicated upon a common equity ratio of 48.00 
percent with a 9.50 percent cost of equity, a long-term debt ratio 51.24 percent with a cost rate of 
3.40 percent, a preferred stock ratio of 0.37 percent with a cost rate of 3.66 percent and a 
customer deposit ratio of 0.39 percent with a cost rate of 0.90 percent for its electric and gas 
operations.57  Further, in the context of a multi-year rate plan, the Company requested a 10.00 
precent ROE with 48.00 percent common equity ratio in RY1, a 49.00 percent equity ratio in 
RY2 and a 50.00 percent equity ratio in RY3.58  In its pre-filed testimony, Staff recommended an 
overall after-tax cost of capital of 5.92 percent for RY1, predicated upon a common equity ratio 
of 48.00 percent with a cost of equity of 8.70 percent, a long-term debt ratio of 51.25 percent 
with a cost of debt of 3.38 percent, a preferred stock ratio of 0.37 percent with a cost rate of 3.66 
percent, and a customer deposit ratio of 0.39 percent with a cost rate of 0.05 percent.59 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends a cost of capital premised upon a 9.00 percent 
ROE with a common equity ratio of 48.00 percent, and a weighted average cost of debt of 3.41 
percent, 3.39 percent, and 3.41 percent for RY1, RY2 and RY3, respectively.  The remaining 
components of the Company’s capital structure, which comprise a relatively small portion of the 
Company’s overall capitalization, are preferred stock and customer deposits.  Overall, the Joint 
Proposal recommends a pre-tax rate of return of 7.66 percent, 7.65 percent, and 7.66 percent for

RY1, RY2, and RY3, respectively, and an after-tax rate of return of 6.08 percent, 6.07 percent,

The ROE and common equity ratio recommended in the Joint Proposal reflect a 
balancing of all the concessions made among the Signatory Parties.  The proposed 9.00 percent 
ROE is 50 basis points lower than Niagara Mohawk’s requested 9.50 percent ROE for a one-year 
rate plan and 100 basis points lower than its requested 10.00 percent ROE for a multi-year rate 
plan.  Additionally, it is comparable to the ROEs authorized by the Commission in recent cases.  
For example, the Joint Proposal’s recommended ROE of 9.00 percent is slightly higher than the 
8.80 percent the Commission adopted in the KEDNY/KEDLI 2021 Order issued in August 2021, 
as well as the 8.80 percent the Commission adopted in the NYSEG/RG&E 2020 Order issued in 
November 2020.  However, since the Commission adopted the multi-year rate plans in those 
proceedings, equity return requirements have generally increased.  Further, as a multi-year rate 
proposal, the rate plan proposed in the Joint Proposal poses more business and financial risk to 
the Company than would a one-year rate plan, and as such investors reasonably require higher 
returns when investing for an extended period of time.  In addition, Niagara Mohawk could be 
faced with additional risk should there be disruptions and volatility in the equity and debt 
markets with the current level of high inflation.  Therefore, the ROE of 9.00 percent is 
reasonable in light of the uncertainty surrounding the impact of these risks at this time.   
Regarding the common equity ratio, a 48.00 percent equity ratio for all three Rate 
Years is reasonable because it is similar to other utilities operating in New York and will allow 
the Company to continue to access capital at reasonable terms.  The overall cost of debt reflects 
the weighted average cost of outstanding long-term debt in addition to the Company’s planned 
long-term debt issuances.  The Joint Proposal’s proposed cost rates for incremental debt are 
reasonable given the current and expected interest rate environment during the Rate Plan. 
2.1.1 Amortization of Excess Deferred Taxes 
The 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act reduced the top federal income tax rate from 35 
percent to 21 percent.  At that time, the Company recorded the effect of the tax rate reduction to 
the Accumulated Deferred Income Taxes (ADIT) with an offset to an income tax regulatory 
liability account.  This represents the customer benefit of the federal income tax rate reduction.  
The Company categorized the excess ADIT between protected plant, unprotected plant, and 


--- Page 30 ---

23 
unprotected non-plant.60  The IRS normalization regulations require the use of the Average Rate 
Assumption Method (ARAM) to calculate the amortization of the excess ADIT balance related 
to protected plant.  Based on the ARAM the Company proposed to amortize the protected plant 
over a period of 46 years and 42 years for its electric and gas businesses, respectively.61  For 
unprotected plant, the Company proposed to amortize the excess ADIT balance using the 
ARAM; however, in an effort to mitigate the electric and gas rate increases, the Company 
increased the amount being amortized by an additional $22.0 million, $10.0 million, and $4.0 
million for RY1, RY2, and RY3, respectively.62  In addition, the Company also reflected the 
amortization that would have occurred under the ARAM method for fiscal years 2019, 2020, and 
2021,63 which the Company proposed be amortized over three years.64  Regarding the excess 
ADIT balances related to the unprotected non-plant, in an effort to further mitigate the electric 
and gas rate increases, the Company did not propose to amortize the balance as it is a regulatory 
asset and thus is an amount to be recovered from customers.65 
In its pre-filed testimony, Staff agreed with the Company’s proposal to amortize 
the excess ADIT balances related to protected plant using an amortization period calculated 
based on the ARAM, which complies with the IRS normalization regulation.  In addition, Staff 
agreed with the Company’s proposal to amortize the excess ADIT balances related to its 
unprotected plant using an amortization period calculated based on the ARAM adjusted to reflect 
an additional amortization amount in each of the respective Rate Years.  Finally, Staff did not 
object to the Company’s proposal to delay the amortization of the excess unprotected non-plant 
to a future rate proceeding.66

equitably balances the return of the excess ADIT to customers in a manner that mitigates the

The Joint Proposal recognizes that, during the term of the rate plan, it is possible 
that the IRS or Congress could provide further interpretation or revisions to tax normalization 
provisions such that the Company would be precluded from the full use of accelerated 
depreciation based on the amortization set forth in the Joint Proposal for protected plant.  In 
order to address this possibility, the Joint Proposal affords the Company the ability to modify the 
accounting for ADIT and the amortization of excess ADIT, only to the extent necessary to 
maintain consistency with applicable tax normalization requirements and to remain eligible to 
use accelerated depreciation without interruption.  The Joint Proposal requires that, if the 
Company determines such a modification is necessary, it must file a notice with the Secretary to 
the Commission.  In such notice the Company must provide an explanation of the change in the 
tax normalization provision and/or the IRS’s interpretation of the tax normalization provision of 
the Internal Revenue Code that results in the need for the Company to modify its accounting for 
ADIT and/or its amortization of excess ADIT related to protected plant.  Further, the notice shall 
include the Company’s proposed modification to avoid a tax normalization violation, the 
materiality of the proposed modification, and the Company’s proposed rate treatment of the

CASES 20-E-0380 et al.

impact of the proposed modification.  This provision is reasonable as a means to ensure that 
Niagara Mohawk avoids any violation of the IRS normalization requirements associated with the 
amortization of excess ADIT that would prohibit the Company from claiming accelerated 
depreciation, including any available bonus depreciation.  Should such a violation occur, it 
would have adverse consequences for customers in the future.  This provision allows the 
Company to proactively address the inadvertent or unintentional situation, if it were to arise, 
prior to any IRS finding of a normalization violation, at which time it would be too late to 
remediate.  By doing so, this provision mitigates the potential incremental cost to customers in 
the future in the event the IRS prohibited the Company from using accelerated depreciation for 
tax purposes. 
2.2 
Shaping of Rate Increases 
 
 
In recognition of the financial impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic on customers 
the Joint Proposal uses deferred credits to shape the overall electric and gas revenue increases 
over the term of the Rate Plan68 in a manner that allows for a gradual transition to full cost-of-
service rates.  The specific amount of credits applied to each of the service classes is shown in 
Appendix 2 and Appendix 3 of the Joint Proposal for electric and gas, respectively.  The shaping 
of the electric and gas revenue increases is reasonable as it allows for a gradual phase-in of the 
cost-of-service rates at a time when many customers may be experiencing financial hardship as a 
result of the COVID-19 pandemic.  Further, the use of deferred credits as the means to shape the 
revenue increases is a reasonable approach since the deferred credits are monies owed to 
customers and thus should be utilized during these difficult economic times to mitigate the bill 
impacts experienced by the customers. 
2.3 
Make Whole Provision 
 
 
The Joint Proposal allows for the Company to recover revenues as if the revenue 
requirement increases were effective as of July 1, 2021, the beginning of RY1.  Accordingly, the 
Joint Proposal recommends that Niagara Mohawk recover the revenue shortfall resulting from 
the extension of the suspension period beyond July 1, 2021 through a make whole provision.  
 
68  The Joint Proposal provides for the use of deferred credits in the stay-out period to further mitigate 
the transition of gas rates to full cost-of-service rates. 


--- Page 33 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
26 
The make whole provision is designed to ensure the Company is restored to the same financial 
position it would have been in had rates gone into effect on July 1, 2021. 
 
 
To accomplish this, the Joint Proposal allows the Company to reconcile the 
difference between the revenues the Company would have received during the extension of the 
suspension period and the actual revenues received.  The revenue adjustment will include all 
applicable surcharges, carrying charges, and be consistent with all applicable reconciliation 
mechanisms.  The proposed make whole provision is reasonable because it restores the Company 
to the same financial position, it would have been in had rates gone into effect on July 1, 2021 
absent the extension of the suspension period, which was necessary to reach a fair and reasonable 
outcome in these proceedings.  The rates contained in the Joint Proposal reflect rate compression 
assuming an effective date of January 1, 2022, offset by the shaping of the rate increases which 
includes the use of deferral credits as previously discussed in section 2.2, above. 
2.4 
Specific Elements of the Revenue Requirement 
2.4.1 Other Initiatives 
 
 
The Other Initiatives O&M expense component includes costs associated with 
proposed incremental FTEs, and non-labor costs of new or expanded programs.  In litigation, 
Niagara Mohawk requested 359.0 FTEs in RY1,69 whereas Staff recommended 200.3 
incremental FTEs in RY1.70  Staff also recommended slipping the start dates by 72 days for each 
incremental FTE. 
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends allowing rate recovery for 243.8 incremental 
FTEs in RY1.  An additional 63 incremental FTEs are included in RY2 and an additional 24 
incremental FTEs are included in RY3.  In addition, it includes a slippage adjustment to the start 
dates for all incremental FTEs by 72 days. The Joint Proposal reflects a reasonable level of 
incremental FTEs given the large reduction from the Company’s litigation position and the 
inclusion of a slippage adjustment for the start dates for all incremental FTEs.  Further 
discussions on certain of the specific incremental FTEs included in the Joint Proposal are 
discussed throughout this Statement.

2.4.2 Site Investigation and Remediation Costs  
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed a RY1 site investigation and 
remediation (SIR) expense rate allowance of $28 million, Company-wide.  Further, the Company 
proposed to allocate 85 percent, or $23.8 million, to electric, and 15 percent, or $4.2 million to 
gas.  The Company based this proposal on an adjusted five-year average of actual spending 
experienced from calendar year 2015 through calendar year 2019, with calendar years 2017 and 
2018 removed from the average.  The Company stated that the removal of calendar years 2017 
and 2018 is warranted as these years were anomalous due to DEC changes to program 
management, significant project manager turnover, and inclusion of other environmental 
initiatives that delayed response/approval of project submittals.73 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff recommended a rate allowance of $21.1 million for 
RY1 with 85 percent, or $17.9 million, allocated to electric and 15 percent, or $3.2 million 
allocated to gas.  Staff also used of a five-year average, but included calendar years 2017 and 
2018.  While calendar years 2017 and 2018 exhibited low spending compared to other years, the

purpose of using a multi-year average is specifically designed to smooth out any fluctuations in

85 percent, or $17.9 million, allocated to electric and 15 percent, or $3.2 million allocated to gas, 
based on a five-year average of actual SIR spending experienced from calendar year 2015 
through calendar year 2019 and is consistent with the approach recommended by Staff.  The 
methodology is reasonable because SIR costs are subject to a high degree of variability, and 
therefore the use of historical spending over a multi-year average should more reliably forecast 
future spending as compared to projected spending forecasts.  In addition, the methodology is 
consistent with the one used in the most recent previous rate proceeding for Niagara Mohawk. 
2.4.3 Total Labor Expense 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company requested management wage increases of 
1.91 percent effective July 1, 2020, and three percent effective July 1, 2021.  The Company 
argued the requested management wage increases were based on market studies and are in line 
with the average wage increases provided over the last ten years.  Additionally, the Company 
indicated that market data supported a 3.25 percent wage increase effective July 1, 2020 but, due 
to the economic impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic, the Company decided to lower the 
requested management wage increase from 3.25 percent to 1.91 percent to reduce the revenue 
requirement impact in these rate filings.75   
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff indicated that under normal circumstances Staff 
would propose management wage increases of 1.42 percent effective July 1, 2020 and 1.88 
percent effective July 1, 2021.  However, given the current economic circumstances as a result of 
the COVID-19, Staff recommended additional austerity measures be established to protect

Proposal reflects a 0.5 percent vacancy rate adjustment to the Niagara Mohawk employee 
headcount, which is reasonable due to the inevitable variability in headcount that occurs 
throughout a given year.

any benefit of the AMR meters and gas ERTs, will pay for them, thus reducing intergenerational 
inequity.  Accordingly, this recommendation is reasonable and should be adopted.

or RY3.   
 
 
In the Joint Proposal, the forecast for RY1 sales is 32,295,901,578 kWh, 
including lighting, or 32,147,262,000 kWh without lighting.91  Based on this estimate, the 
Company is expected to collect approximately $2.67 billion in total RY1 electric revenues, 
which includes delivery revenues, commodity revenues, and gross revenue taxes.  The 
Company’s forecasted sales for RY2 of 32,184,574,315 kWh, including lighting, and for RY3 
31,840,151,315 kWh, including lighting.  Accordingly, the Company is expected to collect 
revenues of $2.74 billion and $2.87 billion in RY2 and RY3, respectively.   
 
 
The total electric revenues agreed to as part of this Joint Proposal are found on 
Appendix 2, Schedules 1.1, 1.2 and 1.3 of the Joint Proposal.  The electric sales and revenue 
forecasts included in the Joint Proposal are within the range of reasonable outcomes in a litigated 
proceeding.  Staff and the Company used similar forecasting models that resulted in nearly 
identical forecasts for both sales and revenues. Accordingly, the Joint Proposal’s electric sales 
and revenue forecasts are reasonable and should be adopted. 
3.2 
Electric Revenue Allocation 
 
 
Niagara Mohawk filed a pro-forma electric ECOS study to support its revenue 
allocation and rate design proposal.92  In the pro-forma electric ECOS study, the Company 
allocated operating costs to the different service customer classes based on an analysis of the 
forecast rate base, operating expenses, and revenues associated with each service class for 
RY1.93  In developing the proposed revenue allocations, the Company’s Electric Rate Design 
Panel considered the costs to provide the type and quality of service required by each service 
class as determined in the ECOS studies.  The Company used the ECOS study to derive a system 
 
89 Exhibit_(E-RDP-2CU), Schedules 2 and 3.

average rate of return, then established a tolerance band of +/-25 percent around the system 
average rate of return.  For classes above the tolerance band, the goal was to bring them to a 
return somewhat above the target return; and for classes below the tolerance band, the goal was 
to bring them to a return somewhat below the target.  Specifically, the service classification (SC) 
1C - Residential Time-of-Use service class had a relative return well above the 25 percent 
benchmark and the Company proposed to give SC-1C a below-average increase.94  It then 
proposed to mitigate extreme price impacts on service classes by spreading rate design changes 
over several years, a widely accepted concept known as “gradualism.”95 
 
 
Staff did not take exception to the use of the Company’s pro-forma ECOS study.  
For each class that was outside of the tolerance band, Staff applied the surpluses or deficiencies 
from the ECOS study, including SC-1C, such that each service class was brought to the edge of 
the tolerance band.96  Staff realigned the RY1 delivery revenues at current rate levels for each SC 
to reflect the deficiencies and surpluses identified in the ECOS studies.  The Staff Electric and 
Gas Rates Panel also allocated the remainder of the adjusted net delivery revenue decreases 
among the SCs in proportion to the relative contribution made by each class to the realigned total 
RY1 delivery revenues.97 
 
 
The Joint Proposal sets forth RY1 electric revenue allocation in Appendix 2, 
Schedule 2.  The Joint Proposal does not attribute that allocation to any particular cost study, 
however it generally uses the Company’s proposed revenue allocation as a guide.  Staff’s 
revenue allocation proposal was similar to that of the Company, and therefore the Joint Proposal 
is reasonable.  Additionally, Appendix 2, Schedules 2.7 and 2.9 show that there is a separate 
allocation of the incremental costs associated with AMI.  Staff finds the separate allocations for 
AMI and revenue requirements for RY1, RY2 and RY3 reasonable as the proposed allocation 
helps to mitigate bill impacts and generally follows cost causation principles.  Because of this, 
the Commission should adopt the Joint Proposal’s electric revenue allocation.

3.3 
Electric Rate Design 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company  proposed  substantial changes to customer 
charges to all classes other than SC-2-ND Small General Non-Demand.98  The Company 
proposed increases to the per kWh charges for the three classes that collect revenue using a kWh-
based charge – SC-1 Residential, SC-1C Residential TOU and SC-2-ND Small General Non-
Demand.99  Likewise, the Company proposed increases to the demand charges for SC-2-Dem 
Small General Demand, SC-3 and SC-3A classes.100  Finally, The Company proposed to 
maintain reactive charges at their current rates.   
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff agreed with the Company’s proposal to keep reactive 
power rates at their current levels.101  Additionally, Staff agreed with the Company’s proposal to 
raise the customer charges for every service class except for SC-1C, as the results of the ECOS 
study suggested such increases were unwarranted.  Staff proposed smaller increases to SC-3 and 
SC-3A customer charges, and consequently larger demand charge increases for those classes.   
MI recommended that the SC3 and SC3A customer charges be increased by at least 50 percent 
more than the corresponding demand charges in this proceeding.102  Walmart also recommended 
that the customer charge for SC3 be set at the cost-based level.103 
 
 
The rate design concepts set forth in the Joint Proposal are essentially the same as 
those proposed by Niagara Mohawk for most service classes, with the exception of SC3 and 
SC3A which had their customer charges increased further as supported by Staff, MI, and 
Walmart.  However, the rates are designed to collect the target revenue determined in the 
revenue allocation process for each service class, and to provide customers with price signals 
that reflect the effect of their usage on the overall system.  The Joint Proposal’s recommended 
electric rates are shown in Appendix 2, Schedule 3 for standard service classes and SC-1 
Voluntary Time-of-Use rates, and shown in Appendix 2, Schedule 5 for SC-7 Standby Rates.  
Typical bill impacts for standard service classes resulting from this rate design are set forth in

3.4 
Modifications to Tariff, Fees and Provisions – Electric 
 
The Joint Proposal requires the Company to make a compliance filing to amend 
its tariffs to implement the rates and other provisions of this Joint Proposal.  Staff recommends 
that these tariff amendments be adopted.  Unless otherwise specified, the tariff modifications set 
forth in this section will be made in the first tariff compliance filing directed by a Commission 
order adopting the terms of this Joint Proposal. 
3.5 
Paperless Billing Credit 
 
 
The electric paperless billing credit consists primarily of the cost of paper, 
postage, and envelopes offset by electronic billing costs.  In the Company’s pre-filed testimony, 
the Company proposed to change the paperless billing credit from the current amount of $0.41 to 
$0.40.104  Staff supported the Company’s proposed paperless billing credit of $0.40.105  No other 
parties had positions.  However, the Joint Proposal updated the costs included in the credit and 
recommends that the paperless billing credit remain unchanged from the currently effective rate 
of $0.41.  The Joint Proposal’s position for the paperless billing credit is reasonable because the 
rate is based on actual billing expenses. 
3.6 
Updated Billing Charges and Billing Back-out Credit 
 
The billing charge is a charge billed to ESCO to issue a consolidated bill (single bill) on 
its behalf.  The Company’s Electric Rate Panel Direct testimony proposed to update the electric 
billing charges to: $0.80 for ESCOs that provide service to an electric-only customer and $0.40 
to ESCOs that supply service to customers that are combined gas and electric customers.106  In 
the Staff Electric and Gas Rates Panel Direct testimony, Staff supported the Company’s 
proposed electric billing charges.107  No other parties had positions regarding the billing charges.  
The billing expenses were updated resulting in the Joint Proposal’s recommendation that the

based on actual billing expenses.   
 
 
Additionally, the Company provides a backout billing credit to a customer that is 
supplied by an ESCO and receives a consolidated bill from either the Company or ESCO.  The 
backout billing credit for the customer is intended to and does match the Company’s billing 
charge to the ESCO.  This ensures that the Company recovers the costs for billing customers 
only one time, from the customer if it is a full-service customer, and from the ESCO if the 
customer receives commodity from the ESCO. 
3.7 
Re-Establishment Charges and Disconnection Fees 
 
 
The Company proposed two changes to its calculations of re-establishment and 
disconnection charges.  First, the Company removed the accounts processing labor costs 
associated with directing the field workers to perform the work to re-establish or disconnect the 
meters.  The Company determined this cost to be minimal and wanted to align the cost 
calculation more closely with its downstate affiliates, KEDNY and KEDLI.  The second change 
was to include Administrative and General burdens in the final fees to be more consistent with 
the addition of overheads for other tariff service fees.108  As a result, the Company proposed to 
assess electric re-establishment meter fees of $54 during normal business hours and $68 after 
normal business hours.  The Company proposed to also assess electric pole disconnect fees of 
$214 during normal business hours and $375 after normal business hours.  Staff supported the 
Company’s proposal.109  No other parties had positions regarding the re-establishment charges or 
disconnection fees.  The Joint Proposal recommends that the Company assess electric re-
establishment meter fees of $54 during normal business hours and $68 after normal business 
hours.  The Company will also assess electric pole disconnect fees of $214 during normal 
business hours and $375 after normal business hours.  The re-establishment charges and 
disconnection fees are reasonable because the credits are based on actual billing expenses for the 
Historic Test Year and as proposed the cost calculation is more closely aligned with its

3.8 
Revenue Decoupling Mechanism 
 
 
Pursuant to the Commission’s 2007 order, Niagara Mohawk has an electric 
Revenue Decoupling Mechanisms (RDM).110  The current electric RDM reconciles actual billed 
delivery service revenues for the RDM reconciliation period, to annual target revenues for 
reconciliation groups.  The RDM reconciliation groups consist of: (i) SC-1 and SC-1C; (ii) SC-
2ND; (iii) SC-15 2D; (iv) SC-3, (v) SC-3A and (vi) Street lighting.  Customers served under SC-
4 and SC-7 are included in the RDM reconciliation group of their otherwise applicable (parent) 
service classification.  SC-12, NYPA load, and qualifying Empire Zone Rate (EZR) and 
Excelsior Jobs Program (EJP) load are exempt.   
 
 
For the electric RDM, the Company proposed only updating the electric RDM 
targets based on its proposed rates and forecasted revenues.111  Staff agreed that the targets 
should be updated based on the ultimately adopted rates and forecasted revenues.112  No other 
parties had positions regarding the electric RDM.  The Joint Proposal recommends that the RDM 
targets be updated consistent with the final revenue requirement and rate design.  Updating the 
electric RDM targets is reasonable because the targets should reflect the most up to date energy 
efficiency targets in order to address potential disincentives that utilities would face as utilities 
engage in energy efficiency programs. 
3.9 
Merchant Function Charge 
 
 
The Company applies the Merchant Function Charge (MFC) to the bills of full-
service customers and includes several components that are designed to recover costs associated 
with commodity-related credit and collections services, commodity-related uncollectible account 
expenses, electric supply procurement costs, and working capital for electric supply.  As 
described in the Company’s pre-filed  testimony, any over/under collections related to electric

3.10 
Efficiency Transition Implementation Plan Exemption 
 
 
The Efficiency Transition Implementation Plan (ETIP) Exemption, exempts 
customers who have load served by the NYPA and SC-12 customers in accordance with their 
individual contracts from being charged with energy efficiency program costs for the NYPA 
portion of their load.  The Joint Proposal requires the Company to update the ETIP Exemption 
credits in the tariffs consistent with the rate design as shown in Appendix 2, Schedule 3 of the 
Joint Proposal.  This provision is reasonable because it updates the rate credits contained in the 
Company’s Tariff by service class, maintaining these customers exemption from ETIP. 
3.11 
Next Base Rate Case 
 
 
The Joint Proposal requires Niagara Mohawk, in its next base rate proceeding, to 
file a historical Embedded Cost of Service Study, however it does not require the Company to 
use the results of such study as part of its rate filing.  This is provision is reasonable because it 
will provide the parties to the next rate proceeding with additional information for consideration 
in revenue allocation and rate design, beyond what would be provided by the Company absent 
this provision.

4.2 
Gas Revenue Allocation 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed to move the revenue produced by 
each service class closer to the class’s revenue requirement as determined in the ECOS study,124 
adhering to the concept known as “gradualism” mentioned above in Section 3.2.  The Company 
established a tolerance band, plus or minus 25 percent of the overall system return at current 
rates, to determine whether each individual service class is over contributing, under contributing, 
or within the tolerance band and applied increases to each service class accordingly.125  Based on 
this, the Company proposed giving SC-1 and SC-5 a slightly higher than average increase, as 
those classes had returns at present rates within the tolerance band and slightly below the 
average.  Further, the Company proposed giving SC-2 and SC-7 a lower-than-average increase, 
as they had returns above the tolerance band.  For SC-8, which had a return of just over zero, the 
Company proposed a larger than average increase, and SC-12 – Distributed Generation, SC-13 – 
Residential Distributed Generation and NYSEG had negative returns and were given a 18 
percent increase.126 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff realigned the RY1 delivery revenues at current rate 
levels for each SC to reflect the deficiencies and surpluses identified in the Company’s gas 
ECOS study.127  Staff allocated the remainder of the adjusted net delivery revenue decreases 
among the SCs in proportion to the relative contribution made by each class to the realigned total 
RY1 delivery revenues. 
 
The Joint Proposal does not attribute that allocation to any particular cost study, 
however it generally uses the Company’s proposed revenue allocation as a guide.  The Joint 
Proposal does reflect certain corrections for errors that were identified in the ECOS study.  After

The rate design concepts set forth in the Joint Proposal are a compromise between

increases as well as volumetric increases while changing the ratios of the tail block to the mid 
block to promote flattening the blocks while minimizing impact to customer bills in RY1.144  The

SC-12 and SC-13, the Joint Proposal recommends increasing the minimum charges only in RY1, 
with correlated changes to the volumetric rates.  For the NYSEG class, the Joint Proposal 
recommends maintaining the current minimum charge and increasing the demand and energy 
charges equally in each of the Rate Years.  The Joint Proposal adopts the SC-7 rate structure 
changes presented in the Company’s testimony and not opposed.  Further, the Joint Proposal 
adopts the SC-6 rate cap as proposed in Staff’s pre-filed testimony.  The rate design contained in 
the Joint Proposal was calculated using a Company rate design model reconciled to Staff’s rate 
design model using the revenue allocation agreed upon in the Joint Proposal.  The Company’s 
revised gas rates are shown in Appendix 3, Schedule 3.1-3.5.  Typical bill impacts for standard 
service classes resulting from this rate design are set forth in Appendix 3, Schedules 4.1-4.4.  
The Joint Proposal’s gas rate design is based on a compromise between the Company’s and 
Staff’s initial positions and is thus a reasonable expected litigated outcome.  Additionally, it is 
based on thoroughly evaluated models.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt the Joint 
Proposal’s recommended gas rate design.

Reliability Surcharge (GSRS) established in the 2018 Rate Order.145  The GSRS allows the

in LPP replacement incremental to the level funded in base rates.  The GSRS allows for recovery

Gas Tariff Modifications 
 
 
The Joint Proposal requires the Company to make a compliance filing to amend

4.6 
Lost and Unaccounted for Gas 
 
 
The Company’s pre-filed testimony proposed to update its lost and unaccounted 
for (LAUF) gas target to reflect the actual experience of the last five annual reconciliation of gas 
expenses and gas cost recoveries (GAC) periods ending August 31, 2019.  The Company stated 
that the methodology is consistent with the Staff Whitepaper on LAUF.147  In pre-filed 
testimony, Staff disagreed with the Company’s proposed LAUF target and associated dead bands 
because the Company’s actual LAUF gas had significantly fluctuated from GAC years 2015 to 
2019.148  Therefore, Staff recommended that the LAUF target be set using the five-year period 
from GAC years 2012 through 2016.  In addition, Staff recommended that Company continue 
implementing an inactive accounts adjustment since this adjustment provides an additional 
incentive for the Company to take action and lock meters for inactive accounts in an expeditious 
matter.149  However, instead of making a volumetric adjustment to the LAUF incentive, Staff 
recommended the Company reduce its revenues in the GAC reconciliation by the inactive 
accounts adjustment, which should be calculated by multiplying the volumes associated with

reasonable because the LAUF target will incentivize the Company to increase the efficiency of 
their systems and are consistent with the Staff Gas LAUF White Paper. 
4.7 
Revenue Decoupling Mechanism 
 
 
The current gas RDM reconciles revenue-per-customer (RPC) target is set for 
delivery service revenues from customers taking service under SC-1, 2, and 7, and actual 
revenues are trued up against the RPC targets annually.  The RDM currently contains RPC 
targets for six groups within these three service classifications: SC-1 - Residential Non-Heat, SC-
1 - Residential Heat, SC-2 - Small General Residential, SC-2 - Small General Commercial, SC-2 
- Small General Industrial, and SC-7 - Small Transportation.  In pre-filed testimony, the 
Company proposed to implement a revenue per class mechanism for SC-1, SC-2, and SC-7.  
Additionally, the Company proposed to establish a new a revenue per class RDM for SC-5 and 
SC-8.151   
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff was generally supportive of modifying the gas RDM 
to a revenue per class basis.  The proposal is in line with the RDMs of other gas utilities such as 
Con Edison, NYSEG, RG&E, KEDNY, and KEDLI.152  However, Staff recommended that the 
Company not expand the gas RDM to SC-5 and SC-8 classes due to the limited number of 
customers in those service classes.153  Since there are so few customers in these classes, and 
since these customers may not always have consistent loads (e.g., a customer may be down for
