maintenance), there is a risk of large RDM imbalances, which could be detrimental to the 
customers in these service classes.  Additionally, the delivery service revenues to these customer 
classes make up only a small portion of the Company’s gas delivery revenues and the Company 
has been at risk for the revenue variations of these customers.154   
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, MI disagreed with the Company’s proposal to expand the 
RDM to include a revenue per class RDM for SC-5 and SC-8.155  MI stated that the Company’s 
proposed “RDM reconciliation process is problematic, especially for groups with fewer and 
larger customers.  Under these circumstances, the gain or loss of a single customer alone could 
trigger a rate adjustment to the remaining customers within the group.”156 
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends Niagara Mohawk modify its RDM for SC-1, SC-
2, and SC-7 to change revenue per customer targets to revenue per class targets.  The Joint 
Proposal identifies RDM targets for each of the Rate Years and the Stayout Period (assuming the 
Stayout Period extends through March 31, 2025).  The Joint Proposal’s RDM provisions 
reasonably reflect the SC-1, SC-3 and SC-7 revenue per class targets.  The shift to a revenue per 
class basis aligns with goals of having customers electrify, rather than use natural gas by 
removing an incentive for Niagara Mohawk to seek new gas customers.  Additionally, the Joint 
Proposal adopts Staff’s and MI’s position and does not establish an RDM for SC-5 and SC-8.  
Because the Joint Proposal’s RDM provisions reflect the recent policy shifts and are the within 
the range of positions put forth by adverse parties, the terms are reasonable and should be 
adopted.

4.9 
Next Base Rate Case 
 
 
The Joint Proposal requires Niagara Mohawk, in its next base rate proceeding, to 
file a historical Embedded Cost of Service Study, however it does not require the Company to 
use the results of such study as part of its rate filing.  This is provision is reasonable because it 
will provide the parties to the next rate proceeding with additional information for consideration 
in revenue allocation and rate design, beyond what would be provided by the Company absent 
this provision. 
4.10 
Paperless Billing Credit 
 
 
The gas paperless billing credit consists primarily of the cost of paper, postage, 
and envelopes offset by electronic billing costs.  In the Company’s pre-filed testimony, the 
Company proposed to change the paperless billing credit from the current amount of $0.41 to 
$0.40.160  Staff supported the Company’s proposed paperless billing credit in the amount of 
$0.40.161  No other parties had positions regarding the paperless billing credit.  However, the 
Joint Proposal updated the costs included in the credit and recommends that the paperless billing 
credit remain unchanged from the current rate of $0.41.162  The Joint Proposal’s position for the 
paperless billing credit, is reasonable because the rate is based on actual billing expenses.

5. 
Computation and Disposition of Excess Earnings 
5.1 
Earnings Report 
 
 
The Joint Proposal requires, by August 31 of each year, the Company file an 
earnings report using the methodology described in this Section and shown in Appendix 4 of the 
Joint Proposal.  The earnings report will set forth the Company’s actual ROE for the preceding 
rate year using a capital structure with a common equity ratio of 48.0 percent.  If the Company 
does not file for new rates to be effective until after June 30, 2024, the earnings sharing threshold 
 
165  Direct Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Gas Rate Design Panel, p. 69. 
166  Direct Testimony Staff Electric and Gas Rates Panel, p. 80. 


--- Page 57 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
50 
of greater than 9.5 percent for any period less than a year before new rates take effect will be 
prorated to develop a stub period earnings sharing threshold.  The stub period earnings will be 
calculated by adjusting the actual average rate base for that period by an operating income ratio 
factor.  The operating income ratio factor will be calculated as the ratio of operating income 
during the same partial period in the previous rate year to the total operating income for that rate 
year. 
5.2 
Discrete Incentives and Revenue Adjustments 
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends earnings adjustment mechanisms, negative 
revenue adjustments (NRAs) and other incentives be excluded from the calculation of Niagara 
Mohawk’s earned ROE, because these items are not directly related to regulated electric and gas 
earnings.  Including these items in the earnings calculation undermine the intended purpose of 
the various incentives. 
5.3 
Earnings Sharing Mechanism 
 
 
In recognition of the possibility that Niagara Mohawk’s earnings could exceed the 
Company’s allowed ROE, the Joint Proposal recommends an Earnings Sharing Mechanism 
(ESM) to capture for the benefit of customers a share of the earnings that exceed certain 
thresholds.  Under the proposed ESM, the Company will calculate the actual earned ROE on an 
annual basis for each of the Rate Years of the rate plan.  Earnings for each of the Rate Years will 
be measured individually, and any overearnings that are owed to customers will be deferred for 
future disposition to be determined by the Commission.167 
 
 
The common equity ratio to be used in the calculation of the actual earned ROE 
will be the equity ratio that was used to develop the cost of capital in the Joint Proposal or 48.0 
percent.168  The Joint Proposal also provides that the calculation of the actual earned ROE shall 
exclude the effects of discrete incentives, amounts previously in excess of earnings threshold, 
and negative and positive revenue adjustments, as discussed in Section 5.3 above.169  An 
example of the calculation for the ESM is shown in Appendix 4 to the Joint Proposal. 
 
167 Joint Proposal, Appendix 4 Schedule 1. 
168  Id. 
169  Joint Proposal, pp. 37 – 38. 


--- Page 58 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
51 
The ESM consists of four distinct tiered bands beginning with a 50-basis point 
“deadband” (first band) above the 9.0 percent allowed ROE.  The Company will retain the 
earnings within this band, i.e., from 9.0 percent to 9.5 percent earned ROE.  For earnings above 
9.5 percent and up to 10.0 percent (second band) the earnings will be allocated equally between 
customers and shareholders.  For earnings above 10.0 percent and up to 10.5 percent (third band) 
earnings will be shared 75 percent/25 percent between customers and shareholders.  Finally, in 
the fourth band, all earnings above 10.5 percent would be shared 90 percent/10 percent between 
customers and shareholders.  The Company will use 50 percent of its shared earnings within the 
third and fourth bands identified above, i.e., its share of earnings above 10.0 percent, to reduce 
regulatory assets related to Site Investigation and Remediation. 
The proposed ESM is reasonable because it provides the Company with a 
financial incentive to control costs while simultaneously ensuring customers have an opportunity 
to share in efficiency gains.  In addition, the use of ESMs is consistent with other multi-year 
plans approved by the Commission.  Further, the 50-basis point deadband is reasonable given 
that it is similar to other ESM deadbands approved in recent cases, including the 
KEDNY/KEDLI 2021 Order and the Con Edison 2020 Order, both of which have a 50-basis 
point ESM deadband.  Additionally, by providing that 90 percent of all earnings equal to or 
greater than 10.5 percent are credited to customers, the ESM provides a significant safeguard 
against the potential for excess earnings by the Company.  The provision for the ESM will 
continue beyond the end of RY3, June 30, 2024, until the Company’s rates are reset in a 
subsequent rate proceeding, thus assuring that customers also would share in any efficiency gains 
realized after end of RY3.

6.3 
Vegetation Management 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed increasing its vegetation 
management program expenditures due to increasing contractor costs and additional Sub-
transmission trimming.194  In pre-filed testimony, Staff agreed that vegetation contractor costs 
had increased in recent years and supported a cycle based sub-transmission program to address 
reliability.  However, Staff recommended that the Company should reduce Emerald Ash Borer 
funding to reflect fewer ash trees in the transmission right of way in RY1.  Staff found that the 
Company exceeded expected ash tree removals in previous years and agreed with the Company’s 
proposal to ramp down funding for the Emerald Ash Borer portion of the vegetation management 
program in the outer years.195 
 
 
The Joint Proposal provides the Company with Vegetation Management funding 
levels that continue proactive trimming and tree removals for improving system reliability with 
the goal of minimizing tree-related interruptions.  The Joint Proposal includes continuing the 
downward-only reconciliation for its combined transmission, sub-transmission and distribution 
program costs, including expenses for the Emerald Ash Borer program, over the term of the rate 
plan.  These terms will ensure the Company has adequate resources for vegetation control and 
should be adopted by the Commission.

6.4 
Major Storms 
 
 
The Company, in its pre-filed testimony, proposed that the rate allowance for 
major storms be based on the most recent ten-year average of major storm expenses experienced 
from calendar year 2010 through calendar year2019; and that the definition of a “major storm” 
be modified for deferability purposes to align it with the definition of a “major storm” for 
reliability purposes.  A major storm for reliability purposes is defined as a period of adverse 
weather during which service interruptions affect at least ten percent of the customers in an 
operating area and/or result in customers being without electric service for durations of at least 
24 hours.196    
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff agreed with the methodology used by the Company 
to determine the rate allowance for major storms and the change in definition of a “major storm” 
for deferability purposes, but proposed certain provisions related to the major storm deferral be 
modified.  Specifically, Staff proposed increasing the deferral threshold, on a per storm basis, 
from its current level of $0.400 million to $0.750 million and eliminating the cumulative major 
storm threshold, i.e., cumulative cost exception.197  The cumulative cost exception allowed the 
Company to defer, for future recovery from customers, the aggregate cost of multiple major 
storm events that did not individually exceed the deferral threshold but when taken collectively 
exceeded $.800 million in one year.  Staff argued the modifications are necessary as the major 
storm reserve is meant for significant storm events.  In addition, these modifications would help 
simplify the determination as to whether an individual storm event met the major storm 
provisions.198 
 
 
The Joint Proposal reflects a major storm rate allowance based on the most recent 
ten-year average of major storm expense experienced from calendar year 2010 through calendar 
year 2019, which is consistent with the Company’s last rate proceeding.  In addition, the Joint 
Proposal adopts the change in definition as to what constitutes a “major storm.”  The change in 
definition of a “major storm” is reasonable as it creates symmetry between the reliability metrics 
and the cost deferability of major storm events and would align the Company with the other 
electric utilities in the state.  
 
196  Direct Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel, pp. 112 – 116. 
197 Direct Testimony of Staff Electric Operations and Infrastructure Panel, pp. 130 – 134. 
198 Id. 


--- Page 65 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
58 
 
 
In addition, the Joint Proposal reflects Staff’s recommended modifications to the 
major storm deferral provisions.  Specifically, during the term of the rate plan, the deferral 
threshold will be $0.750 million to be applied to all qualifying regions, in aggregate, in the 
Company’s service territory for a given storm event and the cumulative major storm threshold 
has been eliminated.  Niagara Mohawk’s deferral threshold is reasonable as it aligns the 
Company with the per storm threshold of other utilities under the Commission’s jurisdiction.  
Further, eliminating the cumulative major storm threshold helps to ensure that the incremental 
costs charged to the major storm reserve are material and related to significant weather events.   
6.5 
Capital Reporting Requirements 
 
 
Niagara Mohawk did not propose any significant changes to the existing electric 
capital reporting requirements set forth in the previous rates case.  These existing reporting 
requirements include the annual and quarterly Transmission and Distribution Capital Investment 
Plan reports, the biennial Distribution System Implementation Plans (DSIP), annual Asset 
Condition Report, and the biennial 15-Year System Plan report.  The Joint Proposal continues 
these reporting requirements, with one minor change.  The Joint Proposal modifies the frequency 
of the Asset Condition Report frequency to biennial instead of the annual submission previously 
required.199  These capital reporting requirements are aligned with Staff’s monitoring, review, 
and tracking goals while enabling transparency with respect to the Company’s progress and 
spending levels associated with electric capital projects, programs and investments. 
6.6 
Non-Wires Alternatives 
 
 
In its pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed two modifications to the 
already-existing Non-Wires Alternative (NWA) procedures and associated shareholder incentive 
mechanism.  First, the Company proposed to change the amortization period over which NWA 
Project costs would be recovered from a set ten-year period to instead match with the project’s 
contract period.200  Second, the Company proposed to eliminate the distinction between “large  
 
 
199 Joint Proposal, Appendix 17. 
200  Exhibit___(EIOP-14), p. 1. 


--- Page 66 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
59 
projects” and “small projects.”201  The Company did not provide an explanation or justification 
for these proposed changes. 
 
 
In its pre-filed testimony, Staff disagreed with the Company’s proposed 
modification to the NWA Project 10-year amortization period, and agreed with the Company’s 
proposal to eliminate the distinction between “large projects” and “small projects.”202  Staff 
noted that although the Company did not justify its proposals, it is reasonable to eliminate the 
distinction between “large projects” and “small projects” because modification in this case 
conforms with the Company’s DSIP filing in Case 16-M-0411, and the updated single definition 
considers all projects with greater than 18 months of lead time and greater than $500,000 in 
CapEx on an equal footing.203  Staff disagreed with the Company’s proposal to modify the ten-
year amortization period because a standard ten-year period provides benefits and avoids 
unintended consequences compared to a shifting amortization period which would potentially be 
different for each NWA Project the Company implements.204 
 
 
The Joint Proposal updates the procedures for NWA projects by maintaining a set 
ten-year amortization period and eliminates the distinction between “large projects” and “small 
projects.”  The Joint Proposal provides an update to the Company’s NWA project procedures 
consistent with the Company’s DSIP, and implements a more inclusive screening criteria for 
traditional infrastructure projects which would have previously been defined as “large projects.”  
The Joint Proposal is reasonable, in the public interest, and within the range of litigated 
outcomes.

7.7 
Bundled Walking, Atmosphere, and Inside Inspection Programs 
In pre-filed testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed to reduce the current five-year 
mains and services inspection cycle to three years to align with the three-year atmospheric 
corrosion inspection cycle.  Niagara Mohawk stated that this alignment would increase the 
efficiency of these types of inspections and reduce associated costs.228  In pre-filed testimony, 
Staff supported aligning the mains and services inspection cycles; and proposed that Niagara 
Mohawk file revised operation and maintenance procedures to codify the three-year inspection 
cycle.229  In its rebuttal testimony, Niagara Mohawk was amenable to revising its procedures 
provided it could revise its procedures in the future should any regulations or inspection 
requirements change.230 
The Joint Proposal recommends that Niagara Mohawk file revised operation and 
maintenance procedures to reduce the current five-year inspection cycle to align with the three-
year atmospheric corrosion inspection cycle.  Also, a provision was added to address future 
changes to the regulations or inspection requirements.  The efficiencies gained by standardizing 
inspection cycles benefit customers by reducing the number physical site visits to the premises 
and in reducing the potential for accessibility concerns.  Thus, the Joint Proposal’s provisions 
relating to inspections cycles are reasonable and should be adopted. 
7.8 
Enhanced Inactive Account Process 
When Niagara Mohawk does not have a customer of record for gas service, it 
needs to shut off the flow of gas to prevent unsafe conditions at the premises.  Niagara 
Mohawk’s current process is designed to resolve inactive accounts within 60 days.  In pre-filed 
testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed to reduce this 60-day period to 30 days.231  In its pre-filed 
testimony, Staff explained that accounts typically remain inactive due to Niagara Mohawk’s 
inability to gain access, the referral of accounts to field operations for physical disconnection, 
 
228 Direct Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Gas Safety Panel, pp. 13 – 16. 
229 Direct Testimony of Staff Pipeline Safety Panel, pp. 58 – 59.

Niagara Mohawk take “immediate and continuous” action to remedy these conditions.234  In its 
rebuttal testimony, Niagara Mohawk challenged Staff’s proposal due to it already taking 
“immediate and continuous” for urgent circumstances.235 
The Joint Proposal recommends that Niagara Mohawk reduce the maximum 
number of days to resolve inactive accounts to 45 days by the end of RY1, to 30 days by the end

reduction from the 60- to 30-day process is a step in the right direction, Niagara Mohawk’s 
future goal should remain to further reduce the number of days to resolve inactive accounts to 
that of “immediate.”  Regardless, by reducing the maximum number of days to resolve inactive 
accounts it benefits customers and the environment by mitigating the potential for theft of service 
which could lead to the unintended release of natural gas to the atmosphere, or worse, within an 
enclosed structure.  Thus, this recommendation is reasonable and should be adopted.

7.10 
Enhanced High Emitter Methane Detection Program 
The Joint Proposal recommends that Niagara Mohawk develop an enhanced high 
emitter methane detection program; hire an advanced leak detection contractor to aid in 
prioritizing for repair large emitting leaks within high concentration areas; and commence 
advanced leak surveying within 12 months of Commission order adopting the terms of the Joint 
Proposal.  As discussed in Section 14.2, below, the leak management metric is reasonable and 
should be adopted due to the decreased threat to public safety and reduced methane emissions 
that will be realized.  By focusing Niagara Mohawk’s efforts on repairing larger emitting leaks 
and conducting more surveys using advanced leak detection technology, there will be increased 
benefits to public safety and further reductions to methane emissions.  For these reasons, this 
recommendation is reasonable and should be adopted.

Each of the incremental safety-related programs benefits the reliability of Niagara 
Mohawk’s natural gas system, customers, and the environment in a variety of ways.  Thus, these 
programs are reasonable and should be adopted.

70 
 
 
Staff did not make any adjustments to the non-IT-related or IT-related O&M 
expenses proposed by the Company but did recommend a downward-only reconciliation for both 
the IT-related and non-IT-related O&M expenses.242 
 
 
The Joint Proposal implements a downward-only reconciliation for both the IT-
related and non-IT-related O&M expenses incurred by the Company during the six-year AMI 
deployment period capped at approximately $119.2 million.  The reconciliation is applicable to 
the majority of cost categories reflected in the Company’s updated AMI Benefit/Cost Analysis 
model.  In addition, to the extent the AMI deployment period is extended beyond six years, the 
Joint Proposal provides for the reconciliation to be extended to reflect the extension of the 
period.  In the event the AMI deployment period is extended the cap will not be modified and the 
Company will continue to use the funds to deliver the scope of work that was intended to be 
completed during the six-year AMI deployment period.  Further, the Company shall notify the 
Secretary of the Commission and explain the circumstances that lead to the need for the 
extension and a status update of the O&M costs incurred to date.  The Joint Proposal does allow 
the Company to seek recovery of costs, in excess of the amounts included in the reconciliation, 
associated with incremental work on AMI implementation to the extent that the Company can 
demonstrate the incremental work and associated costs were both prudently incurred and are 
justified by measurable incremental benefits. 
 
 
The Joint Proposal is reasonable as it ensures that customers only compensate 
Niagara Mohawk for the costs it incurs during the six-year deployment period.  In addition, the 
Joint Proposal incentivizes the Company to control its costs and adhere to its forecasted IT-
related and non-IT-related O&M expense budgets.  Further, it safeguards customers from the 
Company recovering costs in excess of the budgeted amounts unless the Company can 
demonstrate that the costs were prudent and led to incremental benefits for customers.

AMI Opt-Out, Meter Read, and Meter Replacement Fees 
 
 
The Company’s AMI Report initially proposed to assess a monthly meter-reading 
fee for customers who opt-out prior to AMI installation.243  The monthly meter-reading fee 
would be $33.65 for electric-only service, $30.04 for gas-only service, or $46.87 for combined

electric and gas service.244  These fees are the same for customers who opt-out of receiving an

In pre-filed testimony, Staff disagreed with the Company’s proposed meter

$3 per month would be more reasonable – thereby providing a small incentive for customers to 
not opt-out of AMI.”250 
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends that the Company apply the same meter-reading 
fees for customers who opt out of AMI meters as it charges for customers who opt out of AMR 
meters.  The monthly AMI opt-out fees shall be $11.64 for either a gas or electric customer and 
$17.71 for a combined gas and electric meter reading customer.  In addition, the Company will 
charge AMR meter replacement fees of $44.63 for electric, $61.19 for gas, and $89.03 for a 
combined electric and gas customer, and the monthly AMR meter-reading fees will be $11.64 for 
either a gas or electric meter reading and $17.71 for a combined gas and electric meter reading.

the Joint Proposal are reasonable as they are based on Staff’s assumptions for wrench time, travel

9. 
Electric Vehicles 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed three multi-pronged programs 
related to Electric Vehicles (EVs): the Commercial/Multi-User Program (Commercial EV 
Program), the Fleet Program, and the Residential EV Program.  For the Commercial EV 
Program, the Company proposed two components: a Commercial Make-Ready Program 
designed to incentivize development of Level 2 and direct current fast charging (DCFC) EV 
plugs in its service territory, and a Commercial Electric Vehicle Supply Equipment (EVSE) 
Rebate Program to provide rebates to defray the hardware, networking, and other costs of 
installing EVSE to eligible customers.251  The Company proposed to operate its Commercial 
Make-Ready Program in accordance with the Commission’s Order Establishing Electric Vehicle 
Infrastructure Make-Ready Program and Other Programs.252  The Company proposed to include 
forecast Make-Ready Program costs in base rates by treating expenditures related to utility-side 
make ready work as CapEx, treating expenditures related to customer-side make ready work as 
regulatory assets, and recovering program management costs as O&M expenses.253  For its 
Commercial EVSE Rebate Program, the Company proposed to offer rebates equal to 50 percent 
of the average cost of EVSE hardware, networking costs, and other fees associated with 
installation and operation of EVSE for qualifying customers.254  The Company proposed to 
include forecast Commercial EVSE Rebate Program costs in base rates as O&M expense.255 
 
 
For the Fleet Program, the Company proposed three components: a Fleet Make-
Ready Program, an Expanded Fleet Assessment Services program, and rebates for electric school 
buses for low- and moderate-income and environmental justice communities (Bus Rebate

spent basis as defined in the Make-Ready Order, as recommended by Staff.  The list of efforts 
included in this cost recovery mechanism and the three methods for recovering various 
categories of program costs match the programs and cost-recovery mechanisms authorized in the 
Make-Ready Order,302 and constitute the Company’s share of the $701 million of statewide 
funding authorized in the Make-Ready Order.303  Specifically, the Joint Proposal allows the 
Company to defer, as a regulatory asset, electric vehicle-related customer-side program costs, 
incentive costs, and futureproofing costs, amortized over a 15-year period, and to recover the 
costs through a surcharge mechanism.  In addition, the Joint Proposal allows the Company to 
defer, as a regulatory asset, make-ready program implementation costs and fleet assessment 
service costs, amortized over a five-year period, and to recover the costs through a surcharge 
mechanism.  Further, the Joint Proposal indicates that these costs will be excluded from the 
Company’s downward-only net plant and depreciation expense reconciliation mechanism. 
 
 
The Joint Proposal’s close ties to the programs authorized by the Make-Ready 
Order recognizes that the most effective way of rolling out significant policy-related programs in 
a consistent manner across New York State utilities is through purpose-built statewide 
proceedings instead of attempting to achieve the same results through each utility’s temporally 
staggered rate proceedings.  Although parties may argue that the programs authorized in the 
Make-Ready Order and included in the Joint Proposal are not enough to meet New York State’s 
policy goals, those parties have the opportunity to raise such issues, and the Commission will to 
have the opportunity to address those issues, on a statewide-basis as part of the midpoint review 
process already required under the Make-Ready Order.304  In implementing the requirements of 
the Make-Ready Order, the Joint Proposal is reasonable and in the public interest, and should 
therefore be approved.

10.2 
Gas Business Enablement 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed to modify two of the customer 
protections that were established in the 2018 Rate Order.310  First, the Company proposed to 
modify the total project cost cap established in the 2018 Rate Order.  Second, Niagara Mohawk 
proposed to extend measurement period of the Key Performance Indicators (KPI) due to the 
delay of the implementation of the GBE program.311  In pre-filed testimony, Staff  agreed that 
the measurement period of the KPI’s should be extended, but recommended maintaining the total 
project cost cap of GBE should remain at the level set in the 2018 Rate Order.312 
The Joint Proposal recommends maintaining the GBE Total Project cost cap.  
This is reasonable as confirms the commitment to limit the costs of the GBE program established 
in the 2018 Rate Order.  The Joint Proposal also recommends the Company’s proposal, and 
Staff’s acceptance that the KPI measurement periods should be extended due to the delay in 
GBE program implementation.  This provision should also be adopted as the GBE program is 
delayed for reasons partially out of the Company’s control, and therefore, allowing additional 
time to achieve the KPIs is reasonable.

The Company proposed a total replacement of its legacy Customer Service

shift their systems to a new Customer Information System (CIS).313  The Company has utilized 
its current CSS for over 20 years.  Niagara Mohawk’s legacy system uses “technologies that are 
largely unsupported and the required skillsets to maintain and adapt these systems are very

responsible in the event Niagara Mohawk does not implement the CIS project on time and on

project and its cost estimate, then file a petition with the Commission for approval and that costs

proposal recently adopted by the Commission in the KEDNY/KEDLI 2021 Order.

Similar to Staff, UIU proposed that Niagara Mohawk file a petition with the Commission for the

The Joint Proposal excludes O&M and CapEx for the implementation of the new 
CIS.  The Joint Proposal provides that the Company may file a petition with the Commission at 
any time during the rate plan to seek Commission approval of the CIS project and authorization 
to defer CIS-related development costs.  The Joint Proposal specifies that the petition must 
include, at a minimum, a detailed description of the project and related costs, an estimate of the 
costs the Company is seeking to defer, the Company’s sanction papers that demonstrate the 
project and associated costs have been approved by all required Niagara Mohawk management, 
an updated business case, a detailed project implementation timeline, and proposed key 
performance indicators.  Further, the Joint Proposal provides that in such petition, the Company 
will identify the costs and benefits of implementing the features recommended in these 
proceedings by PULP.  This will enable the Commission to determine whether the Company 
should include PULP’s recommended upgrades in the CIS project.  PULP’s recommended 
enhancements to provide residential customers with six years of historic bill transaction, a secure 
elderly, blind-disabled web portal to track coding of these customers, and an enhancement to 
prepare customers for the return of suspended amounts on monthly bills in cases of disputes 
between Niagara Mohawk accounts and the Department of Social Services records.  In the 
absence of Commission deferral authority for the costs of PULP’s proposed CIS upgrades, the 
Joint Proposal does not obligate the Company to include PULP’s upgrades in the CIS project. 
 
 
The CIS provision of the Joint Proposal is reasonable as it seeks to balance the 
need for a new customer system to replace an outdated, problematic legacy system and ensure 
customers are adequately protected and the project is delivered successfully, on budget, and on 
time.  This provision of the Joint Proposal allows the Company and Staff to minimize the risks to 
both customers and the Company, while ensuring the project is vetted, planned, and managed 
appropriately, prior to being approved.  The provision to address the CIS project as a separate 
petition apart from these rate case proceedings is reasonable because it will provide transparency

10.4 
Service Company Rents, IT, and GBE Net Utility Plant and Depreciation 
Expense Reconciliation Mechanism 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed to continue the downward-only 
Service Company Rents Net Utility Plant and Depreciation Expense Reconciliation Mechanism 
that was established in the 2018 Rate Order for the GBE Program capital investments. 324  The 
mechanism requires the Company to reconcile the actual GBE program average net utility plant 
and depreciation expense revenue requirements to the forecast of the revenue requirements used 
to set rates.  In pre-filed testimony, Staff agreed with the Company’s proposal to continue the 
downward-only Service Company Rents Net Utility Plant and Depreciation Expense 
Reconciliation Mechanism, but recommended that it be applicable to both the Service Company 
Rents IT and GBE Program capital investments.325 
 
 
In rebuttal testimony, the Company asserted that a downward-only tracking 
mechanism should be adopted only as part of a multi-year settlement.326  Nevertheless, the 
Company stated that it would not object to adoption of a two-way tracking mechanism that 
would protect both the Company and its customers from differences between the actual IT and 
GBE Program Average Net Utility Plant and Depreciation Expense revenue requirement and the 
level of that revenue requirement used to set rates in these proceedings.327   
 
 
The Joint Proposal includes the continuation of the downward-only Service 
Company Rents Net Utility Plant and Depreciation Expense Reconciliation Mechanism.  
Specifically, each rate year the Company will reconcile its annual IT and GBE Program average 
net utility plant and depreciation expense revenue requirements to the forecast revenue 
requirements set forth in Appendix 5 and Appendix 6 (Schedule 11) of the Joint Proposal.  The 
mechanism is to continue beyond RY3 if the Company is below its target at the end of RY3 and 
will continue until the targets are met.  The Joint Proposal is reasonable as it protects customers 
from paying delivery rates higher than necessary to support the IT assets implemented by the

10.5 
IT Capital Budgeting and Reporting 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed to continue the IT-related reporting 
requirements established in the 2017 Niagara Mohawk Rate Case.  Specifically, the Company 
proposed to continue the following reporting requirements:  annual IT capital investment plan, 
quarterly IT-related variance reports, semi-annual meeting with Department of Public Service 
Staff, and annual long-term IT strategy document.  Further, the Company proposed 
modifications to its quarterly reporting to provide greater visibility to the Company’s IT 
investments.328 
 
 
Staff’s pre-filed testimony proposed modifications be made to the IT reporting 
requirements in collaboration with the Company.  Such modification would be designed to 
improve the IT reporting requirements by streamlining the IT quarterly reporting to provide 
strategic executive-level information regarding the status of the projects and enable easier 
monitoring of project progress in terms of schedule, scope, and budget.  Further, annual IT 
reporting modification should provide a more detailed description of the status of projects and 
address any issues identified.329  Finally, since Niagara Mohawk manages its IT portfolio at the 
Service Company level, the reporting should include the Service Company’s full IT portfolio, 
not just programs that will be allocated Niagara Mohawk.330 
 
 
The Joint Proposal recognizes that the IT reporting requirements require further 
development so that the annual and quarterly reports will provide information in a format that 
permits easy monitoring and understanding of any individual IT project’s status and progress and 
will enable full comprehension of the entire IT portfolio.  Accordingly, the Joint Proposal 
recommends that, following the issuance of the Commission order adopting the terms of the 
Joint Proposal, the Company and Staff will begin a collaborative to develop and improved IT 
annual and quarterly reporting requirements.  The Joint Proposal identified the expectations and

its budgets while also being able to fully comprehend the entire IT portfolio.  The sanction paper

11.8 
The Attachment Process for Smart City Devices 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, NYPA proposed that the Company work with smart street 
lighting technology vendors, municipalities, and NYPA to develop a more streamlined, 
standardized process for the attachment of smart street lighting devices to customer owned 
lights.347  NYPA recommended that the process should pre-qualify smart street lighting devices 
based on certain characteristics such as weight, size, and power draw.  In addition, NYPA 
recommended that the Company designate the pole types capable of attaching the devices based 
on factors such as age and material, and designate the location on the pole, arm, or luminaire 
where the device can be attached based on existing load factors.  Devices meeting the pre-
determined criteria and attaching to pre-approved pole types would be eligible to go through a 
simplified review process, with a reduced engineering review and minimal field inspections.  
Developing a documented, standardized process will facilitate the design process and reduce the 
number of requests to the Company related to smart street lighting technology.  This would allow 
for a faster review and installation process that reduced utility costs and enables projects to move 
forward and start generating energy savings.  recommended that Company facilitate a discussion 
on pole attachment.

11.10 Adaptive Lighting Schedules 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed to establish adaptive schedules that 
define designated total annual hours of operation to represent a reasonable segmentation of 
reduced operating time from the established baseline “normal” (dusk-to-dawn) standard defined 
in the Company’s street lighting tariff (PSC 214).353  These periods of operation, referred to as 
“equivalent” hours, represent cumulative hourly equivalents based on the luminaire’s schedule of 
operation using adjusted power ratings related to lumen output (dimming) and/or part-night 
functionality (off or non-operating periods) for nominal daily operating periods throughout the 
year.  In pre-filed testimony, Staff did not support the adaptive schedules as proposed for 
unmetered street lighting service.354  Staff proposed to bill based on device total wattage and 
operating duration because outdoor lighting currently remains an unmetered electric service.  
The Company’s adaptive schedule proposal does not require any testing to ensure that a 
municipality is adhering to the operating schedule that the municipality submits to the Company.  
Accordingly, Staff stated that “we would be supportive of adaptive lighting if the Company were 
to validate each municipality’s adaptive lighting schedule to ensure that the municipality is 
paying for the energy consumption of the municipality’s street lighting system.”355  In addition, 
Staff recommend that the Company require a municipality to notify the Company in writing of 
any changes to the municipality’s adaptive operating schedule.   
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends the Company offer SC-3 (customer-owned, 
customer maintained) customers four adaptive lighting schedules and undertake measures to 
verify municipal customers’ adaptive lighting schedules.  Customers will be required to either:

11.12  
Street Light Replacement Cost Study 
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends that the Company conduct a street lighting 
luminaire replacement cost study including a review of O&M expense, depreciation, and new 
materials cost versus tariff rates and energy consumption.  This study will address whether the 
Company’s allocated cost study appropriately reflects the cost of asset ownership for the 
Company’s luminaire offerings and as well as the energy usage of luminaries on the system.  The 
Company will provide the results of the study in its next electric rate filing.  This is reasonable 
because it will reasonably inform the parties and the Commission during the next rate base 
proceeding in order to ensure just and reasonable street lighting rates on a prospective basis.

In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed to continue the current

357 Joint Proposal, Appendix 5, Schedule 1. 
358 Joint Proposal, Appendix 6, Schedule 1. 
359  Direct Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Witness Willard, p. 16.

testimony Staff agreed with the Company’s proposal to continue the existing reconciliation

The Joint Proposal recommends continuation of the current SIR reconciliation

12.1.6 Property Tax Expense (Electric and Gas) 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed a full two-way reconciliation 
mechanism through which 100 percent of the difference between its actual property and special 
franchise tax expense and the amount reflect in rates are deferred for future recovery from or 
return to customers.361  In pre-filed testimony, Staff argued that a property tax reconciliation 
mechanism is unnecessary when setting rates for a one-year Rate Plan and that the Company 
would have the opportunity to reflect any material changes to the latest known property tax bills 
later in the proceeding.362  
 
 
The Joint Proposal recommends a property tax reconciliation mechanism through 
which the Company will defer, for future refund to or recovery from customers, the difference 
between the actual property tax expense, excluding the effects of property tax refunds, and the 
rate allowance established in rates.  The difference will be shared 90 percent/10 percent between 
customers and the Company, respectively.  The Joint Proposal is reasonable as it acknowledges 
the difficulty and risk of accurately forecasting property taxes, over which the Company does not 
have complete control, over the term of a three-year rate plan.  In addition, the use of a property 
tax reconciliation mechanism is consistent with prior multi-year rate plans adopted by the 
Commission.  Further, the sharing arrangement between the Company and customers rationally 
shares the financial burden related to the difference in actual property taxes and forecasted 
property expenses between the Company’s shareholders and customers.  This ensures that the

Company will have an interest in controlling property tax expense to the extent it can do so.

12.1.8 Externally Imposed Costs (Electric and Gas) 
 
 
The Joint Proposal includes a provision allowing for 100 percent of all externally 
imposed costs or credits (e.g., externally imposed accounting changes, changes in federal, state, 
or local rates, laws, regulations) associated with, or caused by an individual instance will be  
deferred, once such costs exceeds the threshold of 25 basis points for the year in which the 
change first occurs, calculated and applied separately for electric and gas, and the Commission 
does not otherwise address the treatment of such costs.  In addition, the Joint Proposal lays out 
specific provision regarding Pipeline and Hazardous Materials Safety Administration 
rulemaking, professional engineering stamping, and potential further changes to federal and state 
income taxes.  Should new regulations be promulgated, or legislation enacted, regarding these 
items during the term of the rate plan, the Company will defer all incremental costs or decreases 
in costs arising from such actions without regard to whether such changes exceed the threshold. 
 
 
This provision is reasonable as it recognizes that unforeseen, unknown, or 
uncontrollable actions may occur during the term of the rate plan that have a material financial 
impact on the Company’s costs, revenues, or revenue requirements.  The deferral mechanism 
protects both the Company and customers from these actions during the term of the rate plan and 
therefore should be adopted. 
12.2 
New Electric and Gas Reconciliations, Deferrals, and True-Ups 
12.2.1 Income Tax Repair Adjustment 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony the Company explained that during fiscal year 2009 
National Grid North America adopted a new method of tax accounting to deduct certain book 
capitalized expenditures as routine repair and maintenance expense.  A federal income tax audit 
of National Grid North America determined that a portion of the repair and maintenance expense 
deduction was taken in error, which resulted in the Company paying additional taxes as well as 
interest.  Since the tax benefits associated with repair tax deduction were previously deferred and 
passed back to customers, the Company proposed to defer, for future recovery from customers, 


--- Page 103 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
96 
the interest costs resulting from the subsequent reversal of the repair tax deduction that gave rise 
to the tax benefits.363 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff disagreed with the Company’s proposal to defer the 
interest expense associated with the reversal of the repair tax deduction and argued that the 
Company had not sufficiently demonstrated that customers received the tax benefits associated 
with the repair tax deduction.  Further, Staff indicated that to ascertain whether customers 
received the tax benefits associated with the repair tax deduction, the Company needs to 
demonstrate that, for the time periods it has identified, the deferred tax liability was included in 
the Company’s rate plans and underlying revenue requirements as reduction to rate base.364 
 
 
In rebuttal testimony the Company provided additional documentation to support 
its proposal to defer the interest expense associated with the reversal of the repair tax deduction.  
The documentation included a timeline of events related to the change in tax accounting, a 
detailed explanation of how and when customers received the tax benefits, and supporting 
schedules demonstrating that the benefit was included the Company’s rate plans and underlying 
revenue requirements as reduction to rate base.365 
 
 
The Joint Proposal permits the Company to defer for future recovery from 
customers the interest expense associated with additional income taxes that the Company was 
required to pay as a result of a federal income tax audit related to the repair and maintenance 
expense deductions taken by the Company for fiscal years 2010-2012.  The Joint Proposal is 
reasonable as it reflects that customers received the tax benefits resulting from the deduction and, 
therefore, it is only fair that the Company be allowed to recover the interest expense arising from 
the subsequent reversal of that deduction.  Further, the outcome encourages the Company to 
continue to find ways of lowering its federal income tax expense, which is ultimately recovered 
from customers, by taking advantage of tax deductions available to them.  Accordingly, this 
provision should be adopted.

12.2.3 Energy Storage 
 
 
The Joint Proposal provides the Company deferral authority related to the 
Gilmantown energy storage project.  Specifically, if the Company enters into a contract with a 
third-party energy storage provider for this project, the Company shall defer for future recovery 
from customers the revenue requirement associated with the third-party project and create a 
regulatory liability for the CapEx that are included in the revenue requirement for this energy 
storage project.  Further, the net utility plant and depreciation reconciliation mechanism targets 
shall be adjusted accordingly.  The Gilmantown energy storage project is discussed in further 
detail in Section V.6.2, above. 
12.2.4 Minor Storms 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed a RY1 minor storm expense rate 
allowance based on the Historic Test Year actual expense increased for inflation.  In addition, the 
Company proposed to implement a two-way annual reconciliation mechanism to recover the 
minor storm costs.  Under the Company’s proposal, to the extent that the annual minor storm 
costs exceed the established rate allowance by more than $10 million, the Company would 
recover the amount that exceeds the $10 million threshold from customers and if the annual 
minor storm costs were below the established rate allowance the Company would defer the 
amount below the $10 million threshold for the benefit of customers.366 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff disagreed with both the methodology used by the 
Company to develop its rate allowance and the proposed reconciliation mechanism.  Staff 
proposed a RY1 minor storm expense rate allowance based on the most recent three-year average 
of minor storm expense, calendar year 2017 through calendar year 2019, adjusted for Staff’s 
proposed changes to the major storm provisions.  This treatment would have been consistent 
with recently adopted Commission Orders.  Regarding the proposed reconciliation mechanism, 
Staff argued that a minor storm reconciliation was unnecessary as minor storms are a recurring 
cost that can be forecasted with reasonable accuracy.367 
 
 
In rebuttal testimony, the Company disagreed with Staff’s positions on the rate 
allowance methodology as well as the reconciliation mechanism.  The Company argued that 
 
366 Direct Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel, pp. 116-118. 
367 Direct Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel, pp. 134 – 137. 


--- Page 105 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
98 
minor storm expense has been increasing year over year and presented data demonstrating that 
forecasting future storm restoration costs based on historic levels, without accounting for the 
recent trend of increasing costs, would lead to the minor storm expense being underfunded.  
Additionally, the Company identified the enhanced focus on managing the severity and duration 
of weather-related service disruptions.  By directing more resources to respond to weather 
events, it reduces the chance that significant weather events and the associated restoration costs 
will categorized as a major storm, thereby potentially further increasing expenses categorized as 
related to minor storms.368 
 
 
The Joint Proposal reflects a rate allowance methodology based on an updated 
three-year average, Calendar Year 2018 through Calendar Year 2020, which results in an annual 
rate allowance of approximately $41 million, or, $125.7 million, including inflation, 
cumulatively for RY1 – RY3.  In addition, it establishes a minor storm reconciliation 
mechanism.  Specifically, the minor storm allowance will be subject to a cumulative 
reconciliation such that if the Company incurs less than $125.7 million in minor storm costs, the 
difference between the Company’s actual minor storm costs and the $125.7 million will be 
deferred for return to customers.  If the Company incurs between $125.7 million and $155.7 
million in minor storm costs, i.e., $30 million deadband, no costs will be deferred.  If the 
Company incurs more than $155.7 million in minor storm costs, then 90 percent of costs in 
excess of the $155.7 million will be deferred for future recovery from customers.  Further, the 
reconciliation mechanism will continue during the Stayout Period, but will be a stand-alone 
reconciliation for that nine-month period and will be subject to a deadband prorated for the nine-
month period.  
The Joint Proposal is reasonable because the number of minor storms and the 
related costs experienced in recent years has not only increased but has been unpredictable in the 
Company’s service territory.  The methodology used to establish the minor storm allowance is 
reasonable as it utilizes a multi-year average to account for the year-to-year fluctuation in minor 
storms and is based on the most recent actual minor storm expenditures incurred by the 
Company.  Further, due to the unpredictable nature of minor storms, the increasing trend in both 
the number of minor storms and the related restoration costs, and the importance of restoring 
 
368 Rebuttal Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel, pp. 38 – 47. 


--- Page 106 ---
CASES 20-E-0380 et al. 
 
 
99 
customers electricity in a timely fashion, the Joint Proposal’s minor storm reconciliation is 
reasonable and protects both the Company and customers for any material deviations between 
the established rate allowance and the actual expenses incurred by the Company. 
12.2.5 Pre-Staging Storm Costs 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, the Company proposed that all incremental pre-staging and 
mobilization costs for events that qualify as major storms continue to be charged to the major 
storm reserve.  For pre-staging costs that are incurred in connection with a weather event that 
ultimately does not qualify as a major storm, the Company proposed the incremental costs, to the 
extent that the costs are at least $0.250 million per event be charged to the major storm account, 
up to a maximum of the three largest events per year.369 
In pre-filed testimony Staff agreed with the concept of charging incremental pre-
staging and mobilization costs for storm events that ultimately do not qualify as a major storm to 
the major storm reserve.  However, Staff proposed to modify the amount that could be charged to 
the major storm reserve.  Specifically, Staff proposed costs between $0.250 million and $1.5 
million per event be charged to the major storm reserve, and costs greater than $1.5 million per 
event would be charged 85 percent to the major storm reserve and 15 percent to minor storm 
expense.  Staff argued this is a better way to encourage the Company to prepare for expected 
storm events adequately and reasonably.370 
 
 
The Joint Proposal reflects the Company’s proposal regarding incremental pre-
staging and mobilization costs incurred in connection with a weather event that ultimately does 
not qualify as a major storm, inclusive of Staff’s proposed modifications.  The Joint Proposal is 
reasonable as it allows the Company adequate funding for pre-staging costs and is consistent 
with the other major electric utilities under the Commission’s jurisdiction.

side program costs, incentive costs and futureproofing costs.  In addition, the Joint Proposal 
allows the Company to defer make-ready program implementation costs and fleet assessment

detail in Section V.7.2, above.

The current Residential Service Termination and Uncollectible Expense Incentive

earn positive revenue adjustments if it reduced residential service terminations and/or 
uncollectibles below certain targets, and imposed NRAs if terminations or uncollectibles rose 
above certain targets.

performance mechanism.  Niagara Mohawk claimed that in 2020 it would likely receive a PRA 
largely due to the COVID-19 related suspension of terminations as amended in PSL §§ 32(6), 
89-b(8), 91(9), and 89-l(3).389  The Company stated it will likely be followed by NRAs in 2021 
and 2022 as terminations and net write-offs escalate.390  The Company has not received a PRA 
or NRA since the incentive mechanism was established in the 2018 Rate Order. 
In direct testimony, Staff recommended the Commission pause this incentive 
mechanism in RY1 rather than eliminating the mechanism altogether to avoid artificially inflated 
or deflated target levels that may allow the Company to earn a PRA or incur an NRA without

13.4 
Elimination of Gas Cost Estimating Metric 
 
 
In its pre-filed testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed to eliminate the Gas Cost 
Estimating Metric,400 which was established in the 2018 Rate Order due to Staff’s concerns at 
the time regarding the Company’s ability to estimate the costs of complex gas capital projects.  
Since the metric was established, the Company has improved its estimating processes and has 
exceeded the performance targets adopted in that proceeding.  Because the estimating 
performance issues have been resolved, it is reasonable to eliminate the metric.  As such, the 
Joint Proposal eliminates the metric.  No party recommended the metric be continued.

In its direct testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed more demanding pipeline

performance demonstrates the need for such metrics, and the Joint Proposal further aligns the 
metrics with those in effect for other utilities.

The Joint Proposal recommends calendar year and cumulative LPP removal 
targets; associated positive and negative revenue adjustments; the continuation of utilizing its 
risk-based prioritization algorithm to identify and ranks segments of LPP for removal; the 
continuation of using leak data to prioritize LPP removals; and a provision return to customers 
earned positive revenue adjustments during the rate plan should it failed to achieve a LPP 
removal target in any calendar year or cumulatively.  The LPP removal metric is reasonable and 
should be adopted due to it improving public safety by removing LPP from the natural gas 
system; reducing fugitive methane emissions; and working towards New York climate goals. 
14.2 
Leak Management 
In its pre-filed testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed to reduce its calendar year 
total leak backlog; maintain its calendar year repairable leak backlog; proposed that it could meet 
this metric at any time during the last 10 days of the calendar year to avoid unnecessary 
expenses; proposed associated positive and negative revenue adjustments; and proposed to target 
50 of the 75 highest emitting leaks per calendar year, where possible.407 
In its pre-filed testimony, EDF supported Niagara Mohawk’s proposal to utilize 
advanced leak detection technology and data analytics through its high emitter detection 
program.408  Additionally, EDF argued that Niagara Mohawk is disincentivized to use advanced 
leak detection technology and that the leak management targets and associated positive and 
negative revenue adjustments should be revisited.  Finally, EDF proposed an annual system-wide 
leakage survey using advanced leak detection technology and proposed an alternative positive 
revenue adjustment.409

due to the decreased threat to public safety and reduced methane emissions that will be realized 
from eliminating leaks.

modifying the methodology for calculating this metric; proposed that refresh and relocate one-
call tickets be included in the ticket count; and reiterated its proposal for the targets and 
associated positive and negative revenue adjustments.415

14.4 
Emergency Response 
In its pre-filed testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed to maintain their current 
targets; proposed associated positive and negative revenue adjustments; and to exclude instances 
of 20 or more emergency reports within a two-hour period from issues not caused Niagara 
Mohawk.416  In its pre-filed testimony, Staff supported the continuation of this metric; supported 
the associated positive and negative revenue adjustments; and supported the exclusion clause.417 
The Joint Proposal recommends continuing this metric and the targets; associated 
positive and negative revenue adjustments; the exclusion clause; and maintaining the existing 
parameters surrounding how the response times are measured.  If Niagara Mohawk’s response 
times increase, so does the potential for an incident or safety-related condition to occur.  
Therefore, it is imperative that Niagara Mohawk minimize its response times to emergency 
reports.  Accordingly, this metric is reasonable and should be adopted. 
14.5 
Gas Safety Regulations Performance Metric 
In its pre-filed testimony, Niagara Mohawk proposed alternative targets; a 
reduction to the associated negative revenue adjustment exposure limit; continuation of 
remediation plans for 10 or more violations of a single code section; exclusion of violations 
subject to separate penalty proceedings; and counting multiple violations attributed to an event as 
a single violation.418  In its pre-filed testimony, Staff proposed targets consistent with those of 
other operators; supported a reduction to the associated negative revenue adjustment exposure 
limit; supported remediation plans for 10 or more record audit violations of a single code section; 
 
416 Direct Testimony of Niagara Mohawk Gas Safety Panel, pp. 58 – 59.

and proposed re-including those violations where Niagara Mohawk failed to comply with its

The Joint Proposal recommends targets consistent with those in place for other

revenue adjustments; requiring remediation plans for 10 or more occurrences of a violation of a

disposition.

indicate a lack of control, issues with quality assurance, or a culture that is willing to accept 
violations as a cost of doing business.  Having a metric by which Niagara Mohawk incurs 
negative revenue adjustments for violations serves as an incentive to improve performance and is 
the reason why this metric is reasonable and should be adopted.  Additionally, the associated 
negative revenue adjustment exposure limit should be decreased to balance the exposure more 
appropriately across the Niagara Mohawk’s other pipeline safety performance metrics. 
14.6 
Gas Safety Reporting and Exceptions 
In their pre-filed testimonies, Niagara Mohawk and Staff sought to maintain the 
annual reporting requirement by April 1st of the following calendar year for the pipeline safety 
performance metrics.  The Joint Proposal recommends continuing the annual reporting 
requirement.  These publicly filed reports will allow others to view Niagara Mohawk’s 
performance as it relates to the pipeline safety metrics.  Thus, the reporting requirement is 
reasonable and should be adopted.

Customer Programs 
15.1 
Energy Affordability Program 
 
In light of the COVID-19 pandemic, Niagara Mohawk proposed to modify its 
Energy Affordability Program (EAP) eligibility to include customer self-enrollment to match the 
criteria used at KEDNY and KEDLI: Temporary Assistance for Needy Families, Safety Net 
Assistance – Public Assistance, Supplemental Security Income, Medicaid, Food Stamps, Home 
Energy Assistance Program (HEAP), Veteran’s Disability Pension, Veteran’s Surviving Spouse 
Pension, and Child Health Plus, with the addition of the Lifeline telephone discount program.420  
The Company also recommended consolidating the five discount tiers to four tiers in order to 
limit duplication within the tiers, which provide the same level of discount.421  Additionally, the 
Company proposed to create two new employee positions dedicated to the EAP, a Senior 
Analyst who would assist with reporting and program training and an EAP Enrollment 
Coordinator who be dedicated to EAP outreach and marketing efforts.422  The Company also 
proposed to increase its EAP outreach to inform customers of the EAP, at an annual budget 
amount of $500,000 for the rate term.423 
 
Staff recommended that program eligibility criteria be addressed in the statewide 
generic low income proceeding, Case 14-M-0565, rather than in individual rate proceedings.424  
Staff also proposed that the Company waive all reconnection fees for EAP participants, rather 
than continue the current practice of one lifetime reconnection fee waiver per customer account, 
and that these waivers are funded through the Company’s existing deferral credit balance in 
RY1.425  
 
UIU recommended accepting the Company’s proposed eligibility criteria and 
reiterated its comments provided in Phase Two of the Low Income Energy Affordability 
Proceeding recommending a more sophisticated income verification system.426  UIU also stated

15.1.3 Consumer Advocate Checklist 
 
The Joint Proposal establishes a new training checklist for the Company’s 
Consumer Advocates so they can provide payment options or assistance options to customers 
who have arrears in excess of $300 or who have not made a payment in more than three months 
due to impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic.  These terms are reasonable because they would 
provide customers with a better understanding of these options if they have not been in arrears 
prior to the pandemic and would help those customers avoid service termination after the 
termination moratorium is lifted. 
15.2 
Collections and Special Protections 
15.2.1 COVID-19 Moratorium on Terminations and Disconnections 
 
The Joint Proposal acknowledges the Company will continue to follow Staff’s 
guidance on the COVID-19 Moratorium on Terminations issued in Matter No. 20-01676.  This 
provision should be adopted because it ensures the Company will comply with Staff’s guidance 
on the terminations moratorium. 
15.2.2 Extreme Weather Provisions 
 
 
In pre-filed testimony, Staff proposed that the Commission require Niagara 
Mohawk to automatically provide customers with the cold-weather period protections and to 
develop processes and procedures to dissolve the need for an annual letter to be sent by the 
Director of the Office of Consumer Services.434  Staff also recommended the Commission direct 
the Company to suspend service terminations for non-payment on days that the heat index is

payment during a “heat advisory” as declared by the National Weather Service.  These 
provisions are reasonable and should be adopted because they protect customers from the loss of 
utility service when heating or cooling is necessary for customers’ health and safety during 
periods of extreme cold or heat.

Staff recommended that the Company electronically file its annual outreach and

utilities’ outreach filings.438  The Joint Proposal includes Staff’s recommendations, which are 
reasonable because they increase transparency and allow Staff adequate time for review of the 
outreach plans prior to the summer and winter months.  Therefore, this provision should be 
adopted.

15.5 
Telephone Consumer Protection Act 
The Telephone Consumer Protection Act (TCPA) prohibits non-emergency autodialed 
calls and text messages without customer consent.440  Niagara Mohawk proposed to enhance its 
customer service system to adhere with this federal guidance and will identify accounts with 
incorrect telephone numbers and permit customers to opt-in or opt-out of non-emergency 
messages.  Similar to the implementation of the new Customer Information System, the TCPA 
modifications to the CIS will be conducted at all US National Grid companies.  Given that this is 
a federal mandate, Staff recommended that the TCPA-related modifications be approved, as they 
are in customers’ interests.441   
 
 
The Joint Proposal directs the Company to develop and implement a method for 
adding to its telephone system to comply with the TCPA.  The Joint Proposal discusses the costs 
of Niagara Mohawk’s share of the project totaling, $0.537 million, $0.398 million for CapEx and 
$0.139 million for O&M.  This provision reasonable and in customers’ interest, as it allows for 
more efficient tracking of customer communication preferences and adheres to compliance with 
federal mandates and should be adopted. 
15.6 
Submetering 
In its pre-filed testimony, Staff proposed several improvements to the Company’s 
current submetering process to allow the start of electric service to a building while the customer 
has a pending application before the Commission for submetered electric service.442  Staff 
expects this new process will alleviate construction issues or delays in tenant move-in dates that 
could arise if a Commission order on the petition is delayed and electric service is not 
provided.443  Staff also proposed that the Company charge a temporary volumetric rate and 
require security deposit from these customers until the Commission approves their petition.444

15.7 
Arrears Resolution 
 
The COVID-19 pandemic and associated moratorium on utility service 
terminations has led to increased customer arrears since the beginning of the pandemic in March 
2020.  In response to these increased levels, the Joint Proposal provides that the Company will 
participate in a collaborative commenced by its Downstate Affiliates, KEDNY and KEDLI, and 
file a report.  The Joint Proposal states the Company will request Commission authorization for 
any proposed arrears resolution program, and would not be obligated to undertake any arrears 
programs without the necessary approvals.  The Joint Proposal recognizes that, if the 
Commission makes a determination concerning an arrears resolution on a generic basis, such 
determination will control and obviate need for the collaborative process outlined in the Joint 
Proposal.  This provision is reasonable and should be adopted because it will help to prevent 
widespread service terminations after the conclusion of the termination moratorium. 
15.8 
Language Access 
The Joint Proposal stipulates that the Company will work with Staff and other 
interested parties to assess the Company’s customer communications in different languages, and 
the Company will file a report on these efforts with the Secretary to the Commission in these 
proceedings.  These terms should be adopted because this report will show the various languages

16.

In its direct testimony, the Company proposed two EAM portfolios, one for the

proposed to implement eleven EAM metrics in total: Electric Peak Reduction, Load Factor, DER

Electrification, Transportation Electrification, School Bus Electrification, Electric LMI [Low-

the Savings metrics.447  The eleven metrics proposed by the Company would provide for 
between 80 and 95 basis points of financial incentive for Company shareholders at maximum 
achievement levels depending on the year.448 
 
 
For the Electric Peak Reduction metric, the Company proposed to continue the 
structure of its already-existing metric from the prior rate proceeding,449 with updated targets 
based on the Company’s forecasts of coincident peak demand and associated reductions to the 
coincident peak attributable to various technologies.450  For the Load Factor metric, the 
Company proposed to track load factor at each of eight specific transformers throughout its 
service territory, earning rewards based on the number of transformers either maintained their 
current load factor or experienced an improvement in load factor on an annual basis.451  For the 
DER Utilization metric, the Company proposed to modify the already-existing metric to be based 
on the percentage of the interconnection project queue that is interconnected on an annual basis, 
and by determining the EAM incentive available under the metric using a specified formula on a 
dollar per MW interconnected basis.452  For the Managed EV Charging metric, the Company

Company proposed to implement a new metric to maximize outcome and benefits of the LMI 
portion of its Gas EE portfolio, which would be based on a count-up of lifetime energy savings 
achieved,474 and proposed to implement both annual targets and to all any under- or over-
performance against annual goals to carry over toward achievement of overall three-year term 
goals.475  For the Low Income Geothermal metric, the Company proposed to measure a count-up 
of low income customers that participate in the Company’s proposed Network Geothermal 
Program (discussed in greater detail in Section 18.1, below),476 and proposed to establish both 
annual targets and term-wide targets.477

In its pre-filed testimony, Staff made several general recommendations regarding 
the Company’s EAM proposals overall, numerous recommendations specific to individual 
metrics as proposed by the Company, and made recommendations regarding recovery of earned

of its proposed EAM awards was inaccurate since it did not include the impact basis point 
awards the Company would earn under the Electric and Gas EE Share the Savings metrics in its 
proposed basis point maximum amounts, resulting in corrected maximum annual basis point 
awards of between 104 and 119 for electric and 41 basis points for gas.480  Staff recommended:

gas businesses,481 that the Commission adopt more aggressive targets for EAM achievement,482 
that EAM awards should ultimately be expressed in absolute dollars instead of basis points,483 
and that the EAM metrics, targets, and financial incentives should be set for three calendar years 
regardless of whether this proceeding resulted in a one-year or multi-year rate plan.484  Staff also 
concluded that the Company’s portfolio of EAM proposals was much more heavily skewed 
toward shareholder benefit than for the mutual benefit of both customers and shareholders.  Staff 
observed that the Company maintain metrics unchanged where the Company had historically met 
maximum achievement levels, and that the Company proposed to markedly change or cancel 
other EAM metrics where the Company had historically struggled to meet minimum

under the Joint Proposal are somewhat greater than those proposed by Staff and lower than those 
proposed by the Company.  They are, however, comparable to the level of financial incentives 
available to other utilities.541 
 
 
The portfolio of EAMs included in the Joint Proposal also reflect many of Staff’s 
highest priority recommendations.  Where applicable, baselines for programmatic EAM metric 
achievement are set at the level required by the Commission, with the Company only able to earn 
an EAM based on performance above and beyond such targets.542  The Joint Proposal includes 
baseline and target-setting methodologies for the DER Utilization metric and Transportation 
Electrification metric based on achieving CLCPA and ZEV goals as proposed by Staff.543  The 
Joint Proposal does not include both annual and term-wide targets for any metric as proposed by 
the Company and opposed by Staff.  Additionally, the Joint Proposal does not include the 
Company’s proposed Managed EV Charging metric, which Staff vigorously opposed for a 
panoply of reasons.  The Joint Proposal includes granular cost allocation methodologies based on 
the “beneficiaries pay” principle as proposed by Staff. 
 
 
The EAMs included in the Joint Proposal represent significant compromise 
among the parties, are within the bounds of the testimonial positions of the parties, and will serve 
the public interest by incentivizing the Company to maximize the use of its resources to save 
money for customers, improve the environmental impacts of operating the gas and electric 
distribution systems, and to achieve ZEV and CLCPA policy goals.  Accordingly, the Joint 
Proposal’s EAMs provisions are reasonable and should be adopted.

procedures and take into consideration the long-term reliability concerns associated with the 
Company’s demand forecast, system needs, and specifically with the potential for future hourly

and should be adopted by the Commission.  NPAs are further discussed in Section 18.1, below.

had filed an implementation plan for the 2020/2021 winter season on October 15, 2020, Staff 
noted that any proposal from Niagara Mohawk must stand on its own merit, and that the 
Company had not performed a benefit cost analysis, a vital tool for setting cost-effective 
incentive rates, for its proposed Gas DR program.554  Staff recommended that, instead of 
considering the programmatic details of the Gas DR programs without adequate support in these 
proceedings, the Company should submit a petition along with an implementation plan and draft 
tariff amendments for Commission consideration.555  Staff agreed that the Company should 
include the costs of one of its proposed FTEs in base rates, but that the Company did not need a 
second FTE, a Program Manager, since it can share resources among the analogous programs at 
Niagara Mohawk, KEDNY, and KEDLI.556  Staff disagreed with the Company’s proposal to 
include forecast non-labor O&M costs associated with incentive payments made to customers 
participating in the firm customer Gas DR programs in base rates, and instead recommended that 
such costs be recovered through a surcharge mechanism similar to how such costs are recovered 
for the Company’s Electric DR programs.557

connect to the Company’s gas network.561  The Company also proposed authorization to contract 
for and purchase locally produced biomethane at potentially higher prices than traditional supply 
options and enter into long-term contracts with local biomethane producers.562  Additionally the 
Company proposed to allow producers to retain title to any environmental attribute credits to

17.4 
Multi-Use Hydrogen / Energy Transfer Station Facility 
 
 
In its direct testimony the Company proposed to implement a multi-use hydrogen 
facility, referred to as the Energy Transfer Station (ETS), consisting of a one megawatt MW 
electrolyzer, a compressed hydrogen storage tank, a fuel cell with maximum output of 
approximately one MW, a hydrogen vehicle fuel dispenser, up to four DCFC electric vehicle 
plugs, and a natural gas blending mixing valve.574  The Company proposed that the ETS facility 
could provide hydrogen mixing services into the natural gas distribution system, electrical back-
up to the host site, demand reduction or capacity services to the electrical grid, a low-impact 
source of fast charging for EVs, and directly filling hydrogen-fueled vehicles.575  Additionally, 
the Company envisioned including a bioreactor to produce methane.576  The cost of the ETS 
facility, as proposed by the Company, is approximately $8.7 million in CapEx,577 ongoing O&M 
costs associated with operating the facility,578 and one FTE to manage the project, offset by a 
forecast $0.8 million annually in incremental revenues associated with sale of services produced 
by the facility.579  The Company noted that the ETS facility has the potential to provide 
sufficient revenues to fund all of the facility’s costs, and that the Company would return all net 
revenues generated by the facility to customers until the capital costs of the facility are fully 
recovered, with an 80/20 split of revenues thereafter between customers and between 
shareholders and the Company’s partner, respectively.580  The Company’s partner would have an 
opportunity to buy out the investment in the facility and operate it independently.581 
 
 
In its direct testimony, Staff did not support the Company’s proposed ETS 
facility, and identified three areas of concern.  First, Staff noted concern with the high cost of the 
facility given the magnitude of the revenue and rate increases requested by the Company in these 
proceedings.582  Second, Staff noted that the safety of blending hydrogen into the natural gas

instead replaces such FTE with a contractor for the duration that the Company operates the ETS.
